#### FEATURES OF THE SENIOR HIGH SCHOOL LESSON EXEMPLAR

The Senior High School Lesson Exemplar (LE) is designed to support teachers in delivering meaningful, engaging, and standards-based instruction that responds to the diverse needs of Senior High School learners. Anchored on the Senior High School Instructional Design Framework (SHSIDF), the LE ensures constructive alignment among curriculum standards, teaching and learning experiences, and assessment practices. Through this alignment, learners are provided with opportunities to develop the knowledge, skills, and competencies necessary for higher education, skills development, employment, and entrepreneurship.

At the heart of the LE is the AID Instructional Flow, a structured approach to teaching and learning composed of three interconnected phases: Activating Prior Knowledge, Instituting New Knowledge, and Demonstrating Knowledge and Skills. This instructional flow guides teachers in creating coherent learning experiences that build from what learners already know, deepen understanding through meaningful engagement, and provide opportunities for authentic application and assessment.

The Activating Prior Knowledge phase serves as the entry point of the lesson. It helps learners connect prior experiences and existing knowledge to new concepts, making learning more relevant and meaningful. This phase also establishes the purpose of the lesson by clarifying learning goals, highlighting real-life applications, and motivating learners to actively engage in the learning process.

The Instituting New Knowledge phase focuses on the development of understanding and mastery of concepts. Learners are introduced to new ideas through carefully selected examples, guided exploration, and explicit instruction. Through discussions, collaborative activities, and structured practice, learners progressively deepen their understanding and develop the skills necessary to achieve the intended learning outcomes.

The Demonstrating Knowledge and Skills phase provides opportunities for learners to apply, synthesize, and showcase their learning. Through practical applications, reflective activities, evaluations, and differentiated follow-up tasks, learners demonstrate their understanding while teachers gather evidence of learning. This phase also ensures that learners receive appropriate support through remediation, reinforcement, or enrichment activities based on their individual learning needs.

To further enhance learning experiences, the LE incorporates several key program integrations. Integrative Performance Tasks (IPTs) are embedded and scaffolded throughout the lessons to ensure that learners gradually develop the competencies required to successfully complete authentic and interdisciplinary performance assessments. Rather than being treated as isolated end-of-quarter projects, IPTs are woven into classroom instruction, allowing learners to build the necessary knowledge and skills over time.

Recognizing the demands of a rapidly evolving digital world, the LE also promotes EdTech Integration. Educational technologies are strategically incorporated to enrich instruction, foster collaboration, encourage creativity, and provide learners with engaging and interactive learning experiences.

In addition, the LE strengthens Literacy and Numeracy by embedding literacy and numeracy skills across learning activities and assessments. This integration ensures that learners continuously develop the competencies needed to interpret information, communicate effectively, solve problems, make informed decisions, and navigate real-life situations confidently.

Through these features, the Senior High School Lesson Exemplar serves as a practical and comprehensive guide for teachers, supporting the delivery of high-quality instruction that is learner-centered, inclusive, relevant, and responsive to the aspirations of Senior High School learners and the demands of the 21st century.

-----

#### GUIDE FOR FACILITATING INTEGRATIVE PERFORMANCE TASKS (IPT)

The Integrative Performance Task (IPT) is designed to serve as authentic demonstrations of learning that synthesize competencies across learning areas. To ensure that an IPT becomes the natural culmination of classroom instruction rather than a stand-alone project assigned at the end of a grading period, teachers are encouraged to observe the following principles in the development and implementation of IPT.

First, the Performance Standard should be translated into a concrete, authentic, and real-world product, performance, or solution. Clearly defining the expected output enables teachers and learners to understand the purpose of instruction and maintain focus on the desired end goal throughout the learning cycle.

Second, the accomplishment of the IPT should be developmental and cumulative. Learning Competencies (LCs), technical skills, and disciplinary knowledge required for the final output should be strategically distributed across learning areas and taught within the appropriate lessons. Each lesson should contribute a meaningful component toward the completion of the IPT.

Third, when establishing the purpose of the lesson, teachers should draw from the same real-world context or scenario presented in the IPT. Introducing the scenario early in the learning cycle helps learners recognize the relevance of each lesson and encourages them to continually connect newly acquired knowledge and skills to the final task. The IPT should be presented at the beginning of the quarter and revisited regularly as a reference point throughout instruction.

Fourth, in Finding Practical Applications part of the lesson, teachers should design learning activities that mirror the thinking processes, skills, or performance requirements of the IPT. These formative tasks serve as opportunities for practice and feedback. Whenever possible, teachers should use criteria aligned with the IPT rubric to help learners understand expectations and improve their performance progressively.

Fifth, any digital platforms, educational technologies, mathematical procedures, research methods, laboratory techniques, or other tools required for the successful completion of the IPT should be explicitly taught during the discussion, guided practice, and mastering the concept. Learners should have sufficient opportunities to practice using these tools before they are expected to apply them independently in the IPT.

Sixth, summative assessments should contribute directly to the development of the IPT. Rather than functioning as isolated evaluation activities, these assessments should require learners to produce or refine components of the final output of IPT. This approach strengthens the coherence between instruction, assessment, and performance expectations.

Lastly, the final Lesson Exemplar in the learning cycle should provide opportunities for learners to consolidate, refine, and complete their IPT outputs. This stage may include peer review, consultation, revision, rehearsal, and final polishing of outputs before formal presentation or submission. Teachers should facilitate reflection and feedback to ensure that learners demonstrate both mastery of competencies and meaningful application of learning.

### Unit 1. BUSINESS AND FINANCE

##### I. LEARNING GOALS

| Content | The learners should have knowledge and understanding of business and finance through problems involving earning money, buying, and |
|---|---|
| Standard | selling. |

*The learners demonstrate knowledge and understanding of business mathematics and compensation structures through core payroll computations, commission-based models, piece-rate earnings, and statutory employee deduction guidelines in the Philippines.*

| Performance | By the end of the term, the learners are able to prepare accurate payrolls and digital payslips with correct gross pay, deductions, and net |
|---|---|
| Standard | income. |

*By the end of the term, the learners are able to independently build an automated, professional payroll spreadsheet and digital payslips that accurately calculate gross pay, statutory contributions (SSS, PhilHealth, Pag-IBIG), withholding taxes, and net take-home income.*

| Learning | The learners … |
|---|---|
| Competencies | 1. calculate weekly or monthly wage from an annual salary, wages from an hourly rate including situations involving overtime and other |

allowances and earnings based on commission or piecework.

2. apply percentage increase or decrease in various contexts.
      a. determining the impact of inflation on costs and wages over time.
      b. calculating percentage mark-ups and discounts
      c. calculating VAT.
      d. calculating profit or loss in absolute and percentage terms.
3. solve problems involving salaries, wages, benefits, and deductions (tax computations, overtime pay, and gross and net incomes) using appropriate technology.

3. calculate earnings from commission and piecework; and

Activity A.2. Appreciating Lesson Relevance To facilitate this activity, the teacher may apply one of the suggested strategies in Step 4. You may also include tasks or examples that are familiar to learners and relevant to your local context.

1. Prepare 4 to 5 job role images with short descriptions (e.g., factory worker, teacher, online seller, sales agent, call center agent). Include how each person is paid (salary, per item, per sale, hourly + overtime, profit).
2. Begin the activity by saying: "Let's do a quick game to see what we already know about how people earn money."
3. Post job images on the walls or board.
4. To perform this activity, choose any of the following strategies:
    a. **Strategy 1: Stand and Vote** Ask learners to stand beside the job they think earns more.
    b. Strategy 2: Thumbs Up Polling Learners will show = Yes = No = Not sure
    c. **Strategy 3: Digital Poll**
      - Use application software for instant anonymous voting. (e.g. Mentimeter, Google forms, etc.)
      - Show results live to stimulate discussion.
5. Briefly ask for 1 to 2 volunteers to explain their choice.
6. Connect to the lesson by facilitating the processing questions.
7. End the activity by saying: "Different people earn in different ways. Today, we'll learn how to compute income from various pay types so you can make smart money decisions in the future."

##### 3. Teacher

---

***Job Description: Teachers are responsible for preparing***

lesson plans, educating learners at all levels, assigning homework, grading tests, and documenting learner progress. They instruct in various subjects and aim to reach learners with engaging lesson plans.

***How They Earn: Teachers usually receive a fixed monthly***

salary, which may be determined by their qualifications, experience, and the institution's pay scale.

---

##### 4. Call Center Agent

---

***Job Description: Call center agents handle inbound and***

outbound calls to address customer needs, provide information about products and services, take orders, respond to complaints, and process returns. They are often the frontline of customer service for organizations.

***How They Earn: Call center agents are typically paid an***

hourly wage, with opportunities for overtime pay and performance-based bonuses.

---

##### 5. Sales Agent

---

***Job Description: Sales agents establish relationships with***

new customers, maintain and grow relationships with existing customers, and strive to improve sales. They may work in various industries, presenting and demonstrating products to potential customers.

**How They Earn: Sales agents often earn a base salary plus**

commissions based on the sales they generate. Higher sales typically lead to higher earnings.

---

earns income (fixed monthly salary)? Which structure do you think offers more stability and why?

3. A Sales Agent earns a base salary plus commissions, while a Call Center Agent can earn overtime pay and bonuses. How do these extra earning opportunities change your outlook on a job's potential income?

##### B.2. Discussing the Concept

Activity B.2. Deepening Conceptual Understanding *Instructions: With the guidance of your teacher and support from* your classmates, study the key concepts, formulas, and examples to help you learn how to compute salary, wages, and commission.

##### Key Terms and Formulas Wage means the remuneration or earnings, however designated,

capable of being expressed in terms of money, whether fixed or ascertained on a time, task, piece, or commission basis, which is payable by an employer to an employee under a written or unwritten contract of employment for work done or to be done, or for services rendered or to be rendered.

- Hourly wage is the amount earned for each hour worked.
- Daily wage is the amount of money a worker earns for each day of actual work. It does not include payment for days the employee did not work (e.g., rest days, holidays, or leaves), unless otherwise required by law or employer policy.

##### Salary refers to a fixed, regular payment-typically distributed

monthly, semi-monthly, or weekly-offered to employees regardless of the exact number of hours worked. It is standard for professional, managerial, supervisory, or administrative positions.

- Annual salary is the total gross amount of money an employee earns in a full calendar year before tax deductions.
- Monthly Salary is the fixed amount an employee earns each calendar month.

Annual Salary

Monthly Salary =

12 months

Daily Wage × Factor Monthly Salary =

12 Months (DOLE use the Factor 313 days for a 6-day workweek, and 261 days for a 5-day workweek).

##### A. Explicit Teaching

    b. Prepare printed or digital copies of the problems.
    c. Use a projector or board to show step-by-step computation.
2. Presentation of the Lesson a. Introduce Key Concepts
      - Define wage, salary, commission, piecework, and related terms.

|---|---|---|---|---|

- Show step-by-step computation using real-life figures from the given problems.
- Verbalize your thinking process ("First I determine the regular hours…", etc.).
3. Check for Understanding a. Ask comprehension questions:
- "What's the difference between a daily wage earner and a salaried employee?"
- "How do we adjust pay when overtime is involved?"
4. Practice with Supervision a. Distribute practice problems from the activity set.

- A semi-monthly salary is the amount of money paid to a worker twice a month. By law, the payment intervals must not exceed sixteen (16) days. Monthly Salary Semi­Monthly Salary = 2
- Weekly salary is the income received or computed for a single workweek. Annual Salary Weekly Salary = 52 weeks Weekly Salary = Daily Wage × No. of Workdays per Week

##### Premium Pay refers to the additional compensation required by law

for work performed within the first eight regular hours on nonworking days, such as a scheduled Rest Day, Special Non-Working Day and Regular Holiday. For work performed during regular 8- hour shifts on non-working days, multiply the basic daily rate by the following standard factors from the DOLE Labor Code rules on premium pay:

- Work on a Scheduled Rest Day:

Daily Rate × 1.30

- Work on a Special Non-Working Day:

Daily Rate × 1.30

- Work on a Special Non-Working Day falling on a Rest Day:

Daily Rate × 1.50

- Work on a Regular Holiday: Daily Rate × 2.00
- Work on a Regular Holiday falling on a Rest Day:

Daily Rate × 2.60

- Work on Double Special Non-Working Day:

Daily Rate × 1.50

- Work on a Double Special Non-Working Day falling on a Rest Day:

Daily Rate × 1.95

- No Work on a Double Regular Holiday:

Daily Rate × 2.00

- Work on a Double Regular Holiday:

Daily Rate × 3.00

- Work on a Double Regular Holiday falling on a Rest Day:

Daily Rate × 3.90 b. Provide scaffolding: start with simpler problems, gradually

introduce those involving overtime, commission, and allowances.

5. Provide Feedback: Go over selected problems on the board and correct common errors.

##### B. Guided Discovery

1. Present Key Terms and Formulas
2. Start with a Real-Life Scenario
3. Group Work: Observe and Discover Patterns
    a. Let learners work in groups to solve similar problems (e.g., hourly to weekly wage).
    b. Ask learners to identify patterns:
      - "What changes when the number of days increases?"
      - "How do commissions or bonuses affect income?"
4. Prompt Guided Questions
    a. "What do you notice when someone works overtime?"
    b. "Why do we divide the annual salary by 12 or 52?"
5. Facilitate Sharing
    a. Have groups present their findings or strategies on how they solved a problem.
    b. Write down the discovered formulas and rules on the board.
6. Clarify and Formalize: After learner insights, present the standard formulas and correct misconceptions

##### C. I Do, We Do, You Do

1. I DO (Teacher Models)
    a. Present Key terms and Formulas
    b. Choose one problem type (e.g., overtime computation).
    c. Solve the first example aloud, explaining each step and writing formulas.
2. WE DO (Guided Practice)
    a. Solve the next problem with the class.
    b. Use a think-pair-share format: Let learners first try in pairs, then solve them together.
    c. Ask prompting questions to guide them through:
      - "What is regular pay? How many overtime hours?
      - What is the overtime rate?"
3. YOU DO (Independent Practice)
    a. Assign problems (e.g., 2 to 3 from each computation category).
    b. Let learners solve independently or in pairs.
    c. Encourage showing step-by-step solutions.
4. REFLECTION / WRAP-UP

##### Night Shift Differential (NSD) - often called night differential or

graveyard pay - is the mandatory additional compensation paid to employees who perform work during late-night and early-morning hours. For any work performed between 10:00 PM and 6:00 AM, the employee receives an additional 10% on top of the applicable hourly rate for that day.

Hourly Rate × 1.10

##### Commission Income refers to earnings calculated as a percentage

of total sales. This can be paid as a straight commission or a combination of a base salary plus commission.

##### Piecework Earnings refer to compensation paid to an employee

based on the specific quantity of items or products they complete.

##### Computing Weekly Wages from an Annual Salary

1. An office clerk has an annual salary of ₱312,000. What is his/her weekly wage? Solutions:

##### Computing Weekly Wages from an Hourly Rate

1. A part-time worker earns an hourly wage of ₱100. In one week, he works on a standard 40-hour schedule. What is his total weekly wage? Solutions: Weekly Wage = Hourly Rate × No. of Hours = ₱100 × 40 hours = ₱4,000
2. An employee earns ₱150 per hour and worked 45 hours this week. The first 40 hours are regular time, and 5 hours are overtime on an ordinary day. He also receives a fixed ₱500 weekly allowance. What is the employee's total weekly wage? Solutions:
      A. Regular Pay Regular Pay = Hourly Rate × No. of Hours = ₱150 × 40 Hours = ₱6,000
      B. Overtime Pay Overtime Pay = Regular Hourly Rate × 1.25 × No. of Hours = ₱150 × 1.25 × 5 hours = ₱937.50
      C. Weekly Wage Weekly Wage = Regular Pay + Overtime Pay + Allowance = ₱6,000 + ₱937.50 + 500 = ₱7,437.50
3. A technician has a ₱200 hourly rate. This week he worked 40 regular hours plus 4 hours of overtime on his scheduled rest day. He also receives a ₱1,000 weekly allowance. Overtime on a rest day is calculated at 1.69 times the regular hourly rate. What is the technician's total wage for the week? Solutions: A. Regular Pay Regular Pay = Hourly Rate × No. of Hours = ₱200 × 40 Hours = ₱8,000

-----

B. Overtime Pay

Overtime Pay = Regular Hourly Rate × 1.69 × No. of Hours

C. Weekly Wage

Weekly Wage = Regular Pay + Overtime Pay + Allowance

##### Calculating Earnings from Commission and Piecework

1. A real estate agent earns a 5% straight commission on all sales. This month, he closed a deal worth ₱2,000,000. How much is the agent's commission? Solutions: Commission Amount = Total Sales × Commission Rate
2. A sales representative has a base monthly salary of ₱15,000 plus a 3% commission on all sales made. His total sales for the month reached ₱500,000. What are his total earnings for the month? Solutions: A. Commission Amount Commission Amount = Total Sales × Commission Rate

B. Total Earnings

Total Earnings = Base Monthly Salary+ Commission Amount

3. A factory worker is paid on a piecework basis. He earns ₱20 per unit for the first 500 units produced in a week, and ₱25 per unit for any units produced beyond that as an incentive. This week, he produced 650 units. What is the worker's total earnings? Solutions: A. Base Piecework Base Earning = Base Quota × Base Rate

B. Incentive Piecework

Incentive Amount = (Total Units - Base Quota) × Incentive Rate

C. Total Earnings

Total Earnings = Base Earning +Incentive Amount

+ Overtime Pay +NSD = ₱34,800 + ₱4,160 + ₱2,704 + ₱270.40 = ₱41,934.40

= ₱15,000 + ₱15,000 = ₱30,000

= 500 units × ₱20 = ₱10,000

= (650 units - 500 units) × ₱25 = 150 units × ₱25 = ₱3,750

| Activity C.2. Wrapping Up the Lesson Instructions: Analyze the terms below and sort them into the correct Compensation Box based on what specific event or condition triggers the payment. The Terms to Sort: • Annual Salary • Piecework Earnings • Night Shift Differential (NSD) • Commission Income • Monthly Salary • Overtime Pay • Premium Pay • Hourly Wage Task: Fill the Box Box 1: Time-Based Compensation Trigger: Pay that is tied to a fixed schedule or the mere passage of hours/days. | Activity C.2. Wrapping Up the Lesson Think-Pair-Share Engagement Strategy Instead of running this as a quiet, individual worksheet task, transform it into a collaborative sorting exercise to encourage debate. 1. Give learners two minutes to silently match the terms to the boxes on their own. 2. Have them compare boxes with a seatmate. If they disagree on where a term like "Hourly Wage" or "Overtime Pay" goes, they must justify their reasoning based on the "Trigger" descriptions. 3. Call on different pairs to fill in a box on the whiteboard and explain why they put a specific item there. The Litmus Test Framework Learners sometimes stumble on overlapping categories (e.g., confusing Hourly Wage with Overtime Pay because both involve hours). Teach them to use a simple "Litmus Test" question for each term: • Box 1 Test: "Do I get paid simply because a specific block of time passed on the clock?" (Yes Annual, Monthly, Hourly). • Box 2 Test: "If I sit here for 8 hours and produce absolutely nothing, do I still get paid for this specific item?" (No Piecework, Commission). |

**III. CONTENT Lesson 1.2. Computing Gross and Net Pay**

At the end of the lesson, learners should be able to:

**IV.**

1. differentiate gross pay from net pay;

##### OBJECTIVES

2. use technology to compute tax, SSS or GSIS, Pag-IBIG, PhilHealth contributions, gross and overtime pay; and
3. solve word problems involving gross and net pay.

##### Bridge to Real-World Analysis

Once the boxes are filled correctly, use the completed categorization to drive home a broader lesson on financial literacy and labor economics. Ask the class the processing questions to provoke deeper thought.

*Processing Questions*

1. Between Box 1 and Box 2, which structure offers greater financial stability, and which offers higher earning potential? Explain your reasoning.
2. Why does the law mandate "extra pay" for Box 3 triggers, such as working night shifts, working on holidays, or rendering overtime? How does understanding these three categories empower you to verify if an employer is calculating your payroll fairly and accurately according to the Labor Code?

| Activity B.2. Deepening Conceptual Understanding Instructions: With the guidance of your teacher and support from your classmates, study the key concepts, formulas, and examples to help you learn how to compute gross and net income. Computing Net Income a. Key Concepts and Formulas Gross income is the total earnings before any deductions are made. Includes base salary, overtime, commission, and piecework pay. Gross income = Salary + Allowances Net Pay is also called "take-home pay." This is the amount left after deductions are subtracted from gross pay. Net Pay = Gross Income - Total Deductions Deductions are the amounts subtracted from gross pay by law or agreement. In the Philippines, common deductions include: 1. Social Security System (SSS) Applicable to: Private sector employees Purpose: Retirement, disability, maternity, sickness, and death benefits Rate: Based on a schedule tiered to the employee's monthly gross income. The total SSS contribution rate is 15% of the employee's designated Monthly Salary Credit (MSC). This total percentage is split between the worker and the company: ● Employee Share: 5% ● Employer Share: 10% SSS has strict boundaries that dictate how wages are categorized. The Minimum Cap (Income Floor): ₱5,000.00 MSC - If an employee's gross monthly salary is less than ₱5,250.00, their wage defaults to the lowest bracket base of ₱5,000.00. - Absolute Minimum Employee Share: ₱5,000.00 × 5% = ₱250.00 The Maximum Cap (Income Ceiling): ₱35,000.00 MSC | Activity B.2. Deepening Conceptual Understanding To effectively deliver this part of the lesson, you may use any of the suggested strategies below. Take note of the corresponding tips to enhance implementation. A. Explicit Teaching 1. Prepare Necessary Materials a. Prepare visual aids with key terms and formulas b. Prepare printed or digital copies of the problems c. Use a projector or board to show step-by-step computation 2. Presentation of the Lesson a. Introduce Key Concepts • Gross income • Net pay • Deduction b. Present the sample problem and demonstrate the use of Spreadsheet to compute net pay 3. Check for Understanding a. Ask comprehension question: • How can technology help us compute faster and more accurately? • What happens if the gross income increases? • How can technology help us compute faster and more accurately? B. Guided Discovery 1. Present Key Terms and demonstrate the use of spreadsheet 2. Start with a real-life scenario presented 3. Group Work: Observe and discover patterns. Let learners work in groups to solve the remaining problems 4. Prompt Guided Questions: a. How can technology help us compute faster and more accurately? b. What happens if the gross income increases? c. How can technology help us compute faster and more accurately? C. I Do, We Do, You Do |

- If an employee earns an exceptionally high salary (e.g.,

₱50,000.00 or ₱100,000.00), their premium computation hits the maximum bracket base of ₱35,000.00. - Absolute Maximum Employee Share: ₱35,000.00 × 5% =

₱1,750.00 Step-by-Step Payroll Computation To determine an employee's exact deduction during payroll processing, follow this workflow:

1. *Calculate the Gross Monthly Earnings: Sum the base salary* and any taxable allowances or overtime pay received during the month.
2. *Find the Income Bracket: Locate the official SSS Contribution* Table and find where the gross income fits along the "Range [of Compensation" column. https://www.sss.gov.ph/wp-](https://www.sss.gov.ph/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/2025-SSS-Contribution-Table-rev.pdf) [content/uploads/2024/12/2025-SSS-Contribution-Table-](https://www.sss.gov.ph/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/2025-SSS-Contribution-Table-rev.pdf) [rev.pdf](https://www.sss.gov.ph/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/2025-SSS-Contribution-Table-rev.pdf)
3. *Identify the MSC: Look at the corresponding Monthly Salary* Credit (MSC) assigned to that specific bracket.
4. *Multiply by the Employee Rate: Multiply that specific MSC by* 5% to find the exact salary deduction.

##### 2. Government Service Insurance System (GSIS)

*Applicable to: Government employees only* *Purpose: Life insurance, retirement, disability, and survivorship* benefits *Rate: 9% of monthly salary rounded to the nearest peso (employee* share) Under Republic Act No. 8291 (the GSIS Act), mandatory monthly contributions are computed as a direct percentage of the employee's Actual Monthly Basic Salary (MBS). The total mandatory contribution rate is 21%, split as follows:

- Employee (Personal) Share: 9% of the actual Monthly Basic Salary.
- Employer (Government) Share: 12% of the actual Monthly Basic Salary. There is no maximum salary ceiling or income cap for regular GSIS members. Additionally, there is no fixed statutory "floor amount." GSIS membership is compulsory for all government personnel who receive compensation and hold a recognized appointment, regardless of their employment status. This includes regular employees and casual employees. Job Orders (JO), Contract of

    c. Solve the first example aloud, explaining each step of solving the problem using the problem
2. WE DO (Guided Practice)
    a. Use a think-pair-share format: Let learners first try in pairs, then solve together
    b. Ask prompting questions to guide them through:
      - How did you compute the SSS, PhilHealth, and Pag-IBIG?
      - What is the net pay?
3. YOU DO (Independent Practice)
    a. Assign problems (e.g., 2-3 from each computation category)
    b. Let learners solve independently or in pairs
    c. Encourage showing step-by-step solutions
4. Reflection / Wrap-Up a. Review key takeaways: common deductions on the salary, how to determine these deductions, how to encode formulas in spread sheet. Synthesize the discussion by asking the Processing Questions.
1. Which specific step or calculation rule in the payroll workflow did you find the most challenging or surprising to compute during the activity?
2. How does a person's monthly basic salary, rather than their total gross pay, dictate the actual deduction amounts for mandatory contributions like GSIS and PhilHealth?
3. Why is it conceptually important to subtract non-taxable statutory contributions from an employee's gross income before calculating their monthly withholding tax?

-----

Service (COS) workers, and uniformed personnel are not covered by GSIS.

##### 3. Pag-IBIG Fund (HDMF)

*Applicable to: All employees* *Purpose: Housing loans, savings, and calamity loans* *Rate: 2% of monthly salary rounded to the nearest peso (employee* share). Pag-IBIG (HDMF) has a strict minimum baseline and a maximum cap. The parameters are governed by Pag-IBIG Circular No. 460, which outlines the mandatory contribution rules based on an employee's Monthly Fund Salary (MFS) or Regular Income. *The Maximum Fund Salary cap is exactly ₱10,000.00.*

- Any income earned above ₱10,000.00 is completely ignored for the mandatory calculation.
- Because the standard rate for incomes over ₱1,500 is 2%, the maximum deduction is locked: - Maximum Employee Share: 10,000.00 × 2% = ₱200.00 - Maximum Employer Share: 10,000.00 × 2% = ₱200.00 - *Total Maximum Remittance: ₱400.00* *The Minimum Cap*
- There isn't a "floor" that forces low-income earners to pay a higher flat amount (unlike PhilHealth's ₱10,000 floor). Instead, the minimum rules change the percentage rate applied to actual earnings: - Employees earning ₱1,500.00 or less per month qualify for a reduced Pag-IBIG deduction rate of 1% of their monthly basic contract pay. The employer is still legally required to pay a matching share of 2%. Despite the set cap, Pag-IBIG allows employees to voluntarily contribute extra money beyond the standard limit to build up personal savings and retirement investments.

##### 4. PhilHealth

*Applicable to: All employees* *Purpose: Healthcare benefits and hospitalization coverage* *Rate: 5% of monthly salary, shared between employee and* employer The total premium rate for PhilHealth Contribution is 5% of the Monthly Basic Salary (MBS) or the Regular Income, which is split

-----

equally (2.5% each) between the employee and the employer. However, the calculation is strictly bound by these limits: *The Minimum Cap (Income Floor): ₱10,000.00*

- If an employee earns less than ₱10,000.00 a month, their contribution is calculated as if they earned exactly ₱10,000.00. - Minimum Total Premium: ₱500.00 per month - Minimum Employee Share: ₱250.00 *The Maximum Cap (Income Ceiling): ₱100,000.00*
- If an employee earns more than ₱100,000.00 a month, their contribution is capped exactly at the ₱100,000 limit. - Maximum Total Premium: ₱5,000.00 per month - Maximum Employee Share: ₱2,500.00 Republic Act No. 11223 (Universal Health Care Act) is the current legal mandate that explicitly governs the progressive increase of PhilHealth premium collections up to the 5% rate along with the designated salary floor and ceiling limits.

##### 5. Withholding Tax (BIR)

*Applicable to: All employees* *Purpose: To deduct and remit an employee's income tax liabilities* progressively *Rate: Based on monthly salary bracket and applicable exemptions* for tax rate. Refer to this link [https://mpm.ph/bir-tax-](https://mpm.ph/bir-tax-table/#incometaxtable) [table/#incometaxtable](https://mpm.ph/bir-tax-table/#incometaxtable) The tax charged directly to a person's income, salary, or wages is called the Withholding Tax on Compensation (often referred to broadly as Individual Income Tax). It is called a withholding tax because the employee doesn't have to manually pay it to the Bureau of Internal Revenue (BIR) every month. Instead, the employer is legally mandated to deduct (withhold) the tax directly from the employee's paycheck and remit it to the government on their behalf. The Philippines uses a progressive tax system regulated by the TRAIN Law (RA 10963- Tax Reform for Acceleration and Inclusion). Under this system, those who earn more are taxed at higher percentage rates, while lower earners are exempted entirely. The tax is calculated using a structured tiered table.

-----

##### Tax Payroll Bracket/ From To Fix Tax Rate on Frequency Column Excess

| 1 | 0 | 20,833 | 0 | 0 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2 | 20,833 | 33,332 | 0 | 15% |
| 3 | 33,333 | 66,666 | 1,875 | 20% |
| 4 | 66,667 | 166,666 | 8,541.80 | 25% |
| 5 | 166,667 | 666,666 | 33,541.80 | 30% |
| 6 | 666,667 | and above | 183,541.80 | 35% |

Step-by-Step Payroll Computation

1. Determine Gross Compensation Income: Base Salary + Overtime + Allowances.
2. Deduct Non-Taxable Items: Subtract the employee's statutory contributions (SSS or GSIS, PhilHealth, and Pag-IBIG shares). *These are strictly non-taxable.*
3. Compute Taxable Income: Taxable Income = Gross Income - (SSS or GSIS + PhilHealth + Pag-IBIG)
4. Apply the Bracket Table: Look up the Taxable Income amount in the monthly tax bracket table to calculate the exact deduction.

##### 6. Other Deductions (Optional or Conditional)

These deductions depend on agreements between the employee and employer

- Loans
- Cash Advances
- Insurance Premiums
- Union Dues
- Deductions for Absences or Tardiness

**b. Computing the Net Income or Net Pay** ***Examples***

*Problem 1.* Anna works in a factory and earns ₱550 per day. She worked for 22 days this month. What is Anna's gross income for the month? Considering her deductions for SSS, PhilHealth, Pag-IBIG, and tax, compute her net income for the month. *Solutions*

| Items | Computation | Amount (₱) |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Gross Income | 550.00 × 22 | 12,100.00 |
| 2. SSS | 12,000.00 × 5% | 600.00 |
| 3. PhilHealth | 12,100.00 × 2.5% | 302.50 |

-----

| 4. Pag-IBIG | Max: 200.00 | 200.00 |
|---|---|---|
| 5. Tax | Exempted | 0.00 |
| Deductions | Sum of items 2 to 5 | 1,102.50 |
| Net Pay | Gross Income - Deductions | 10,997.50 |

Note:

- A Gross Income of ₱12,100.00 falls under a Monthly Salary Credit (MSC) of ₱12,000.00. To compute Anna's SSS Personal Share, multiply the MSC by 5%. (Refer to the 2025 SSS Contribution Table *under SSS Circular No. 2024-006).*
- In computing the PhilHealth premium, the total 5% contribution is divided equally between the employee and the employer. Therefore, Anna will only contribute her personal share of 2.5%.
- The Pag-IBIG employee share is computed at 2% of the monthly compensation but is strictly capped at a maximum of ₱200.00. *Problem 2.* James earns ₱75 per hour. He worked 160 regular hours and 10 hours Over Time (125% rate). What is James's total gross income, including OT? Considering his mandatory government deductions, compute his net income for the month. *Solutions:*

| Items | Computation | Amount (₱) |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Regular | 160 × 75.00 | 12,000.00 |
| 2. Overtime | 10 × 75.00 X 1.25 | 937.50 |
| 3. Gross Income | Sum of items 1 and 2 | 12,937.50 |
| 4. SSS | 13,000.00 × 5% | 650.00 |
| 5. PhilHealth | 12,000.00 × 2.5% | 300.00 |
| 6. Pag-IBIG | Max: 200.00 | 200.00 |
| 7. Tax Withheld | Exempted | 0.00 |
| Deductions | Sum of items 4 to 7 | 1,150.00 |
| Net pay | Gross Income - Deductions | 11,787.50 |

Note:

- A Gross Income of ₱12,937.50 falls under a Monthly Salary Credit (MSC) of ₱13,000.00. To compute Jame's SSS Personal Share, multiply the MSC by 5%. *Problem 3.* Ms. Rivera is a public-school teacher in the Philippines with a Salary Grade (SG) 22, Step 1. She receives a monthly salary of ₱71,511.00

-----

and non-taxable allowance of ₱2,000.00, but she also has deductions including taxes, contributions, and a GSIS loan of ₱5,000 per month. Find her net pay after all deductions. *Solutions:*

| Items | Computation | Amount (₱) |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Regular Salary | Given | 71,511.00 |
| 2. Allowance | Given | 2,000.00 |
| 3. Gross Pay | Sum of items 1 to 2 | 73,511.00 |
| 4. GSIS | 71,511.00 × 9% | 6,435.99 |
| 5. PhilHealth | 71,511.00 × 2.5% | 1,787.78 |

6. Pag-IBIG Max: 200.00 200.00 1,875.00 + (Taxable Income -
7. Tax 7,825.85 33,333.00) × 20%

| 8. Loan | Given | 5,000.00 |
|---|---|---|
| Deductions | Sum of items 4 to 8 | 21,249.62 |
| Net Pay | Gross Pay - Deductions | 52,261.38 |

Note:

- The contributions for GSIS, PhilHealth, and Pag-IBIG are computed strictly based on the Monthly Basic Salary, not the Gross Pay. Allowances, such as the ₱2,000.00 ACA/PERA, are non-taxable and excluded from these computations.
- Under the TRAIN Law, the withholding tax is determined using the Taxable Income (Basic Salary minus mandatory GSIS, PhilHealth, and Pag-IBIG contributions).
- Since the taxable income for this specific bracket falls between ₱33,333.00 and ₱66,667.00, the tax is computed as a fixed ₱1,875.00 plus 20% of the amount exceeding ₱33,333.00.
- The Pag-IBIG employee share is computed at 2% of the monthly compensation but is strictly capped at a maximum of ₱200.00.

**III. CONTENT Lesson 1.3. Applying Percentages in Various Contexts**

**IV.** By the end of the lesson, learners will be able to: **OBJECTIVES** 1. explain the concept of percentage increase and decrease;

2. apply percentage calculations in various contexts such as inflation, VAT, discounts, and profit/loss; and
3. solve problem involving percentage.

##### Percentage in the Various Contexts

##### 1. Understanding Percentage Change

a. *Introduction to Percentage Increase and Decrease* Percentage change (PC) indicates the extent to which a quantity has increased or decreased in relation to its original value. It is often utilized in finance, sales, budgeting, and data analysis.
b. Formula for Computing Percentage Change

New Value - Original Value

Percentage Change =

Original Value

c. *Determining Percentage Change Direction*
    - If the result is positive, it indicates a percentage increase.
    - If the result is negative, it indicates a percentage decrease.

To deepen learners' understanding, facilitate the discussion of the following topics using the suggested steps. You may include additional examples as needed

1. Organize learners into small, mixed-ability groups to encourage peer support and discussion.
2. Provide a learner-friendly handout that explains the key concepts. Allow groups time to read, discuss, and collaborate to ensure everyone understands the material.
3. Create task cards featuring real-world problem sets aligned with the concepts in the handout. Each group will apply what they've learned to solve these scenarios.
4. After solving the tasks, have each group present not only their × 100 answers but also what their solution means in a real-life context.
5. Encourage learners to relate the problems to their own live such as budgeting at home, school fundraisers, or small business ideas to deepen understanding and relevance.

-----

d. Interpreting Positive and Negative Percentages

- +15%: The value increased by 15% from the original.
- -10%: The value decreased by 10% from the original. Examples:
1. An item was priced at ₱600 last week. Today, it is being sold for ₱650. By what percentage did the price change, and in what direction? Given: Original Value= ₱600 New Value= ₱650 Percentage Change=? Direction=? Solutions:

New Value - Original Value

PC = × 100

Original Value

650 - 600

= × 100

600 = 8.33% *Direction: Percentage Increase*

2. A stock's value dropped from ₱120 to ₱90. By what percentage did the value change? Given: Original Value= ₱120 New Value= ₱90 Percentage Change=? Direction=? Solutions:

New Value - Original Value

PC = × 100

Original Value

90- 120 = × 100

120 = -25% *Direction: Percentage Decrease*

##### 2. Inflation and Its Effects

a. *What is Inflation?* Inflation is the general increase in prices of goods and services over time, resulting in decreased purchasing power.
b. *How Inflation Affects the Cost of Goods* When inflation occurs, everyday items like rice, bread, or gasoline become more expensive, meaning your money buys less.

-----

c. *Adjusting Wages for Inflation*

To maintain the same standard of living, wages need to increase at the same rate as inflation (or keep pace with inflation). If wages stay the same while prices rise, people will effectively earn less. *d. Rate of Inflation*

The Inflation Rate measures the percentage change in the overall price level of a country, typically tracked using the Consumer Price Index (CPI) over a specific period (usually a year). *e. Formula*

CPICurrent Year -CPIPrevious Year Inflation Rate = × 100

CPIPrevious Year *f. Understanding the Consumer Price Index (CPI)*

CPI represents the average price of a standard "basket" of goods and services (like food, housing, transportation, and utilities) consumed by households. Examples

1. The average Consumer Price Index (CPI) of the Philippines was 120.0 in 2023 and rose to 124.8 in 2024. What is the country's inflation rate for 2024? Given: CPIPrevious Year = 120.0 CPICurrent Year = 124.8 Inflation rate =? Solutions:

CPICurrent Year - CPIPrevious Year

Inflation Rate = × 100

CPIPrevious Year

124.8 - 120.0 = × 100

120.0 = 4.0%

2. If the Consumer Price Index (CPI) for food items jumped from 115 to 122 due to agricultural supply shortages, what is the food inflation rate? Given: CPIPrevious Year = 115 CPICurrent Year = 122 Inflation rate =?

-----

Solutions:

CPICurrent Year - CPIPrevious Year Inflation Rate = × 100

CPIPrevious Year 122 -115 = × 100

115 = 6.09%

3. Mark-ups and Discounts in Business
a. *Difference Between Selling Price and Cost Price* Cost Price (CP): The original amount a business pays to buy, manufacture, or produce a product. Selling Price (SP): The final retail price tag set for the consumer. This amount must cover the cost price plus operational expenses (like rent and salaries) to yield a profit.
b. *Computing Mark-up Percentage* The Mark-up Percentage (MP) indicates how much a business increases the cost price to determine its retail selling price. Selling Price- Cost Price Mark - up Percentage = × 100 Cost Price The top part of the formula (Selling Price - Cost Price) is simply the Mark-up Amount in cash value.
c. Calculating Final Prices after Discounts A discount reduces the selling price. To find the final price (Discounted Price or DP) in a single step, multiply the original selling price by the remaining percentage (1- Discount Rate or 1 - r) the customer actually has to pay.

Discounted Price = Selling Price × (1 -Discount Rate)

*Examples:*

1. If the cost price of an item is ₱50 and its selling price is ₱75, then what is its mark-up percentage? Given: Cost Price= ₱50 Selling Price= ₱75 Mark-Up Percentage=? Solutions: SP - CP MP = × 100 CP 75- 50 = × 100 50 = 50%

-----

2. If the cost price of a material is ₱200 and is mark-up to 30%, then what is its selling price? Given: Cost Price= ₱200 Mark-Up Percentage= 30 Selling Price=? Solutions:

= × 100 ~~100 (~~ - × 100)

=

100 ×

+ = - + 100

× = +

100 30 × 200

= + 200

100 = 260

3. A product is discounted to 20%. If its selling price is ₱1,000, then what is its discounted price? Given: Selling Price= ₱1,000 Discount Rate= 20% Discounted Price=? Solutions: DP = SP(1 - r) = 1000(1 -0.20) = 1000(0.80) = 800
4. Value Added Tax (VAT) a. *Introduction to Value Added Tax (VAT)*
    - VAT is an indirect consumption tax levied on the sale, barter, exchange, or lease of goods, properties, and services in the Philippines, as well as on items imported into the country.
    - Under the National Internal Revenue Code, the standard tax rate is 12%.
    - It is an indirect tax. The consumer bears the economic burden of the tax, but businesses act as collection agents that remit the funds directly to the national government.
    - VAT-Inclusive Price: The final retail price tag shown to the customer, which already includes the 12% tax. In the

-----

Philippines, businesses are legally required to display VATinclusive prices on price tags.

- VAT-Exclusive Price: The base value or net price of an item before the 12% tax is added. This represents the actual revenue the business keeps for itself. b. *Converting Between VAT-Inclusive and VAT-Exclusive Prices* VAT can be separated or added using these mathematical formulas:
- Finding the Price Without VAT (VAT-Exclusive Price): To strip the 12% tax out of a retail price tag, divide by 1.12. VAT­Inclusive Price VAT­Exclusive Price = 1.12
- Finding the Retail Price with VAT (VAT-Inclusive Price): To add the 12% tax to a wholesale or net baseline price, multiply by 1.12.

VAT­Inclusive Price = VAT­Exclusive Price × 1.12

*c. Computing the VAT Amount*

To determine the exact cash value of the tax collected for the government, use either of these methods.

- Subtraction Method VAT Amount = (VAT­Inclusive Price) - (VAT­Exclusive Price)
- Direct Percentage Method VAT Amount = (VAT­ Exclusive Price) × 0.12 *Examples:*
1. If the VAT-Inclusive Price is ₱112, then what is the VAT- Exclusive Price and the VAT Amount? Solutions: VAT­Inclusive Price VAT­Exclusive Price = 1.12 112 = 1.12 = 100 VAT Amount = (VAT­ Exclusive Price) × 0.12 = 100 × 0.12 = 12
2. If the VAT-Inclusive Price is ₱560, determine the VAT-Exclusive Price and the VAT Amount. Solutions: VAT­Inclusive Price

VAT­Exclusive Price =

1.12

-----

=

1.12 = 500 VAT Amount = (VAT­ Exclusive Price) × 0.12

= 500 × 0.12 = 60

3. If the VAT-Exclusive Price is ₱1,200, then what is the VAT Amount and the VAT-Inclusive Price? Solutions: VAT Amount = (VAT­ Exclusive Price) × 0.12 = 1,200 × 0.12 = 144 VAT­Inclusive Price = VAT­Exclusive Price × 1.12 = 1,200 × 1.12 = 1,344

1. Learners or groups solve one problem on chart paper or whiteboards.

##### Differentiated Problem Sets

1. Create three sets of problems aligned to the same learning goal:
    a. Basic - Focuses on foundational skills
    b. Intermediate - Involves moderate challenge and reasoning
    c. Advanced - Includes complex, real-life, or multi-step problems
2. Assign or let learners choose a problem to solve.
3. Explain the activity's purpose.
4. While learners work, circulate and offer guidance or feedback.
5. Lead a short class discussion.

### Unit 2. SEQUENCE AND SERIES

##### I. LEARNING GOALS

**Content** *The learners demonstrate knowledge and understanding of ...* **Standard** *a. patterns, arithmetic and geometric sequences.*

*b. financial application of sequence and series*

| Performance | The learners are able to describe and present how they use sequences and series to make informed decisions on real-life situations involving |
|---|---|
| Standard | earning money, buying and selling, acquiring loans, making investments, and planning purchases. |
| Learning | The learners… |

**Competencies** 1. describe patterns by inspection including patterns in art and nature.

2. determine the next term of a given pattern, including Fibonacci sequence.
3. identify the rule of a given pattern.
4. illustrate the attributes of arithmetic and geometric sequences.
5. solve problems involving arithmetic and geometric sequences.
6. differentiate sequences from series.
7. illustrate arithmetic and geometric series.
8. represent series using sigma notation and vice versa
9. find the sum of arithmetic and geometric series.
10. solve problems involving arithmetic and geometric series.
11. apply sequences and series to solve financial problems such as periodic payments for loans and mortgage.
12. determine the accumulated values of investment over time using sequences and series.

##### III. CONTENT Lesson 2.1. Sequence

At the end of the lesson, learners should be able to:

1. identify patterns found in art and nature;
2. describe a sequence;

##### IV. OBJECTIVES

3. classify sequences as finite or infinite based on their structure and number of terms;
4. determine the next term of a given sequence, including a Fibonacci sequence; and
5. describe the rule for finding the next term in a sequence.

*Instructions:*

1. The class will be divided into small groups.
2. Each group will watch a documentary video showcasing an animal's life cycle (for example: the transformation of caterpillar into a butterfly)
3. While watching, take note and/or sketch each stage of the animal life's cycle. Pay attention to the sequence, pattern and characteristics of each stage.
4. After the video, collaborate with your group to create a visual presentation-such as a poster, flipbook, or digital slideshow-illustrating the sequential stages of the observed life cycle. Presentation can also be done using Microsoft PowerPoint or Google Slides or Canva.
5. Presentation must include: a. Title and animal chosen

Strategy 1. Collaborative Work

1. Begin by explaining the objective: to understand sequencing by observing the stages of an animal's life cycle.
2. Show a documentary video depicting the life cycle of a specific animal. Encourage students to watch attentively, noting the sequence of stages.
3. Introduce sequencing words such as "first," "next," "then," and "finally." Discuss how these words help describe the progression of events or stages.
4. Divide students into small groups. Instruct each group to create a visual representation (e.g., poster or storyboard) of the animal's life cycle, using sequencing words to label each stage. Encourage creativity.
5. Have each group present their visual representation to the class. Facilitate discussion by asking questions such as:

1. Print the sequences and gather pens.

12. Have groups post to Padlet or visual aids to present their work.
13. Have group representatives explain their answers to the class.

  b. 3, 6, 9, 12, \_\_\_
  c. **5, 10, 20, 40, \_\_\_**

17. Ask groups to write a reflection answering: "How did you find the *pattern and missing terms?"*

| Activity A.2. Appreciating Lesson Relevance: Pattern Hunt Instructions: 1. Pictures from nature and artwork featuring striking images that highlight patterns will be shown through a slide show. 2. Observe the pictures and look for repetition, symmetry, shapes, or structure. 3. Discuss your observations with a partner, then share your insights with the class. Processing Questions: 1. What mathematical patterns (such as repetition, symmetry, or sequence) did you observe in the pictures and artworks? | Activity A.2. Appreciating Lesson Relevance: Pattern Hunt To facilitate this activity, the teacher may apply one of the following strategies: Strategy 1. Hear Me 1. Prepare a slideshow featuring various pictures captured from nature and art. 2. Let students observe the pictures carefully, noting details such as symmetry, repetition, shapes, and structure. 3. Have them discuss their observations with a partner, then share their insights with the class. 4. Connect to the lesson by asking students to give more examples of patterns they see in nature. 5. After sharing, say: "Just like we see patterns in nature and art, we can find patterns in numbers too. These number patterns are called sequences. Today, we will describe sequences of numbers through observation, inspecting what changes and what repeats-just like what we did with the images." Strategy 2. Pattern Gallery Walk 1. Post printed images from nature and art around the room. 2. Divide students into small groups and have them visit the posted images. 3. Ask them to describe the patterns they see using terms like repeating, increasing, decreasing, and symmetry. 4. Connect to the lesson by asking students to give more examples of patterns they observe in nature. 5. After sharing, say: "Just like we see patterns in nature and art, we can find patterns in numbers too. These number patterns are called sequences. Strategy 1. Interactive Formula Mapping

1. Prepare sets of cards:

-----

##### Key Terms and Definitions Sequence - a list of numbers arranged in a specific order,

following a rule. Example: 3, 6, 12, 24, . . .

**Term - each number in a sequence.**

Example: In sequence 3, 6, 12, 24, each of the numbers 3, 6, 12, and 24 is a term.

##### Position (or Index) - the place of a term in the sequence, usually

| counted as | 1st term, 2nd term, | and so on |
|---|---|---|
| Notation: | 1 is the first term, | 2 is the second term, etc. In |
| general, | denotes the nth | term |

##### Rule (or Pattern Rule) - a description of how the term is formed

or how they change from one to the next. Example: "Add 2" in the sequence 2, 4, 6, 8, …

##### Fibonacci Sequence - a sequence where each term after the first

two is the sum of the two preceding terms. Example: 1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 13, …

##### Classification of Sequences (based on the number of terms) Finite Sequence

A finite sequence has a specific number of terms - it has a beginning and an end. You can count all the terms. It stops after a certain point. Example: 2, 4, 6, 8, 10 (This sequence has 5 terms - it ends at 10)

##### Infinite Sequence

An infinite sequence has no end- it goes on forever. It keeps following the same rule endlessly. You can describe the rules, but you can't list all the terms. Example: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, … (This keeps doubling and never stops.) In mathematical notation, an infinite sequence often includes ellipsis "…" to show it continues. *Processing Questions:*

Set A: Key terms Set B: Definitions Set C: Examples

2. Distribute shuffled cards to small groups.
3. Groups must correctly match all three: term, definition, and example.
4. After the given time, review answers as a class. Strategy 2. Word Hunt Instructions
1. Prepare cards with terms and definitions written on them.
2. Place the cards around the room or distribute them randomly.
3. Divide students into small teams.
4. Each team searches for cards that match terms with their corresponding definitions. When they find the correct pair, they return to the teacher for confirmation.
5. After all cards are matched, discuss each pair with the class, ensuring everyone understands how the definitions and terms connect.
6. Practice with examples using the definitions. Strategy 3. Peer Teaching Instructions
1. Create teaching stations for each term with its definition.
2. At each station, prepare:
3. The definition for that term.
4. An example related to the term.
5. Split the class into small groups and assign each group to a station.
6. Each group explains the term, definition, and example at their station.
7. After the given time, rotate the groups to the next station.
8. When all groups have visited each station, bring the class together to discuss any challenges or findings. Strategy 4. Differentiated Guided Practice (Teacher-Led Small Group Instruction) Facilitation Guide: 1. Group learners based on their performance in the previous activity or assessment. 2. For the remediation group, revisit the basic concept of sequences (e.g., identifying patterns through addition or multiplication) using simple examples and step-by-step explanation. 3. Strategy 2. Station Rotation Activity (Paper-Based + Optional EdTech Integration) *Facilitation Guide:*

1. Prepare 2-3 stations before class: Station 1 (Remediation): Basic sequence drills (identify patterns, fill in missing terms, guided examples). Station 2 (Core Practice): Mixed-level problems for independent practice. Station 3 (Enhancement): Higher-order tasks such as creating sequences, solving real-life problems, or explaining patterns.
2. Divide learners into small groups and assign rotation schedules (e.g., 10-15 minutes per station).
3. Provide clear written instructions at each station so students can work independently.
4. Monitor and give support where needed, especially for remediation learners.
5. End the activity with a quick whole-class sharing or reflection.

Optional EdTech Integration: Use Google Forms or Quizizz at one station for immediate feedback practice

Part I. Pattern Detectives the Rule

1. Prepare the Materials
2. Arrange learners into groups with 4-5 members.
3. Assign roles within the group.
4. Introduce the Activity by saying: "Today, you will become Pattern Detectives. You will analyze number patterns, discover rules, and explain how numbers are related. Your observations will help us understand sequences and series."
5. Present the Number Patterns
6. Give the Group Task:
    a. identify the next missing term
    b. determine the rule or pattern
    c. explain how the pattern changes
    d. prepare a short explanation for class sharing
7. While learners are working:
    a. move around the classroom
    b. observe participation
    c. ask guiding questions such as
    - "What do you notice about the numbers?"
    - "How does one term change to the next?

BLUE CARD for Sequence

3. Discuss the correct answers briefly.

-----

The teacher will flash a set of numbers on the screen. Raise: BLUE CARD → if it is a sequence

GREEN CARD → if it is a series

Examples

3,6,9,12,… 3+6+9+12+… 2,4,8,16,… 2+4+8+16+…

5. Conclude the activity by saying: "The patterns you analyzed today are examples of mathematical relationships. Some represent sequences, while others can form series when terms are added together. These concepts will help us learn how to represent series using sigma notation."

*Processing Questions:*

1. What is the next term in Pattern C? How did you determine your answer?
2. Which patterns show numbers increasing? Which show numbers are decreasing?
3. Compare Pattern A and Pattern B. How are they similar and different?
4. How can you tell whether a set of numbers follows a rule or pattern?
5. Suppose you create your own pattern. What rule would you use, and how would you explain it to your classmates?

| Part 1. Group Formation |  |
|---|---|
| and Roles | 1. Before the Activity (Pre-Gaming) |
| Form groups of 4-5 members. Quickly assign the following roles: |  |
| The Architect (Leader): Manages the workflow and ensures everyone participates. The Scribe (Recorder): Writes down the data and final patterns on the Manila paper. The Voice (Presenter): Speaks on behalf of the group during the presentation. The Tracker (Timekeeper/Encourager): Keeps the group on schedule and keeps energy high. Part 2. The Discovery Phase Step 1. Analyze the Data Your group has been assigned one of the following lists of numbers. Study it carefully. Group A: 2, 4, 6, 8 Group B: 1, 3, 5, 7 Group C: 5, 10, 15, 20 Group D: 2, 4, 8, 16 Task 1: On your Manila paper, describe the pattern of your list. What is the rule to get to the next number? Step 2: The Accumulation Challenge Imagine these numbers represent daily monetary savings, blocks built on a tower, or points scored per round in a game. Day 1: You get the 1st number. Day 2: You get the 2nd number. Day 3: You get the 3rd number. Day 4: You get the 4th number. Task 2: Calculate the grand total accumulated by the end of Day 4. On your Manila paper, write out the exact mathematical expression showing how you combined these numbers to get that final total. | • Crucially, do not use the words "series," "addition," or "sum" during your introductory remarks. The power of this lesson depends entirely on inductive discovery. Refer to the mathematical representations initially as "lists of numbers" or "ordered sequences." • Distribute activity materials, Manila paper, and markers to each team. Ensure that the "Action Cards" (the large numbers, plus signs, and equals signs) are stacked face- down on the tables, so groups do not guess the trajectory of the mechanics prematurely. 2. During the Activity (Monitoring & Scaffolding) Part 2. The Discovery Phase • Circulate around the classroom. Ensure that groups are identifying the underlying arithmetic or geometric operational shift between terms (e.g., adding a constant 2, or doubling the preceding value). • If a group struggles to construct the mathematical expression for their accumulation total, prompt them with: "If you had to write out the exact, raw calculator strokes needed to prove your total to another team, what sequence of keys would you press?" You want them to write out the full additive framework (e.g., 2 + 4 + 6 + 8) rather than jumping directly to the single numerical value. Part 3. The Living Equation • Direct the students responsible for inserting the operational sign cards (+ and =) to move into position silently. These deliberate silent dynamic forces the rest of the observing class to focus visually on the structural transformation taking place. |

-----

Part 3. The Living Equation

1. Each group will stand up and physically build their mathematical expression for the class using their Action Cards.
2. Four students will hold up the individual daily numbers, standing in order from left to right.
3. Without speaking, other group members must step in and place the + and = signs between the students where they belong.
4. The final student will reveal the grand total.

#### 5. The Voice will explain: "Our pattern was increasing by [X]. Our

grand total is [Y]."

Part 4: Guided Inductive Analysis (Processing Questions) *Look at the work of all groups displayed on the board. Answer the* *following questions together as a class or in your groups to define* *today's math concepts.*

Phase A. Distinguishing Sequence vs. Series

1. Look at how the numbers were originally listed in Step 1 (separated by commas) versus how they looked in Step 2 and the presentations (separated by plus signs).
    - What is the structural difference between a list of ordered numbers and the total accumulation of those numbers?
    - If the comma-separated list is called a Sequence, how would you define a Series in your own words based on what you just did?
2. Complete this sentence: *"A sequence is a list of numbers, whereas a series is the \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_* *of those numbers."*

Phase B. Deepening the Concept

1. Compare the sequence 2, 4, 6, 8 and the expression 2 + 4 + 6 + 8.
    - Which one is a set of individual coordinates/points?
    - Which one results in a single, static value? What is that value?
2. Real World Connection: Think of a movie. A sequence is like watching individual frames of a movie one after the other. What part of the movie experience is like a series? (Think about the total runtime or the plot building up).
- Enforce tight pacing to maintain high energy. Keep each group's physical presentation concise, targeting a maximum window of 1.5 to 2 minutes per team.

##### 3. After the Activity

Part 4. Guided Inductive Analysis (Processing Questions) This structured reflection is where the conceptual definitions are formalized. Guard against the urge to lecture; extract the structural rules directly from their hands-on experiences.

- **Phase A (Sequence vs. Series Transition): Draw the class's** attention to a static sequence on the chalkboard next to a group's completed living equation.
    - Ask the class: "If a sequence behaves like individual, isolated cars spaced out along a highway, how does a

series change that behavior?" Lead them toward identifying terms like accumulation, collective summation, or building a unified structure.

- **Phase B (Deepening the Structural Contrast): Lead** students to articulate clearly that a sequence serves as a list of distinct independent positions, whereas a series computes an active process that resolves into a single, unified numeric solution.
- **Phase C (The Bridge to Sigma Notation): Use the "Lazy** Mathematician" thought experiment to establish an immediate logical bridge into your next module. Have two or three groups draw their invented shorthand notations on the board.

##### The Instructional Hook:

*"I see that writing out 100 terms of an addition pattern is tedious. It turns out that mathematicians centuries ago ran into the exact same frustration, so they adopted a specific Greek letter-Sigma (Σ)-to act as a structural command block. Tomorrow, we are going to learn how to code your patterns directly into it."*

-----

##### Phase C. Bridge to Sigma Notation

1. Suppose your sequence didn't stop at 4 terms. Suppose it went on for 100 terms (2, 4, 6, 8, ..., 200).
    - Writing 2 + 4 + 6 + 8 + ... + 200 takes too much time and space.
    - Brainstorm as a group: If you had to invent a shortcut symbol or a shorthand way to tell someone to "add up the first 100 terms of a pattern that starts at 2 and goes up by 2," what would that symbol or notation look like? Sketch your idea!

##### Key Terms and Definitions Series - the sum of the terms of a sequence.

*Example:* For the sequence 1, 3, 5, the series is 1 + 3 + 5 = 9

**Finite Series - a series with a definite number of terms.**

*Example: 2 + 4 + 6 + 8 (only 4 terms)*

**Infinite Series - a series that continues without end.**

*Example: 1 + ½ + ¼ + 1/8 + …*

The nth term of a sequence is usually denoted by

**Partial Sum (Sn) - the sum of the first n terms of a sequence.**

If the nth term of the sequences is

= 1 + 2 + 3 + . . .+ *Example: In the sequence, 1, 3, 5, 7, 9, . . .,*

S4 = 1 + 3 + 5 + 7 = 16

is the nth term of the series

**Sigma Notation (∑) - a shorthand symbol used to express a series.**

To facilitate discussion, apply any of these strategies. Strategy 1. Inquiry-Based Learning

1. Display a sequence (1, 3, 5) and its corresponding series (1 + 3 + 5) without explanation.
2. Ask learners to observe silently for 1 minute and ask guiding questions:
    - "What do you notice?"
    - "What changed in the second expression?"
3. Allow think-pair-share (2-3 minutes).
4. Call volunteers to share answers.
5. Guide learners to define:
    - sequence
    - series
6. Introduce finite and infinite series using examples.
7. Explain an and Sn with visual illustration.
8. Confirm correct understanding through questioning.
9. Introduce the sigma notation.

, then Strategy 2. Teacher-Led Instruction

1. Present sequence and corresponding series.
2. Define key terms clearly:
    - sequence
    - series
    - finite series
    - infinite series
3. Introduce an, Sn , and sigma notation.

##### III. CONTENT

At the end of the lesson, learners should be able to:

1. illustrate sequence and series;
2. define arithmetic sequence and series and their key components;
3. find the nth term of an arithmetic sequence;

##### IV. OBJECTIVES

4. use the appropriate formula
5. solve real world problem involving arithmetic sequence and series; and
6. demonstrate appreciation of the importance of arithmetic sequences and series by explaining or presenting how they are used in making informed decisions in real-life situations.

Each number in the sequence is called a term.

##### Series

A series is the sum of the Example: 2 + 4 + 6 + 8 + 10

##### Term

A term refers to each individual element in a sequence or series.

Example: In the sequence 3, 7, 11, 15, …

- 3 is the 1st term
- 7 is the 2nd term
- 11 is the 3rd term

##### Arithmetic Sequence

An arithmetic sequence is a special type of sequence in which the difference between any two consecutive terms is constant. This constant difference is called the common difference (d). *Example: 5, 8, 11, 14, 17, …* Observe the pattern: 8 - 5 = 3 11 - 8 = 3 14 - 11 = 3

Since the difference between consecutive terms is always 3, the sequence is an arithmetic sequence with: d = 3

##### Important Parts of an Arithmetic Sequence

*First Term (a1)* The first term is the starting value of the sequence. Example 1. 7, 10, 13, 16, … The first term is: a1 = 7 *Common Difference (d)* The common difference is the constant number added to each term to get the next term. Example: 12, 17, 22, 27, …

7. Connect each concept to real-life applications such as earnings, population changes, and savings.

Find the difference between consecutive terms: 17 - 12 = 5 Thus, d = 5

*nth Term (an)* The nth term refers to the term found in the nth position of the sequence. To determine the nth term of an arithmetic sequence, use the formula: an = a1 + (n -1)d where:

- an = nth term
- a1 = first term
- d = common difference
- n = position of the term Example 2. Find the 10th term of the arithmetic sequence: 5, 8, 11, 14, …

Solution: Step 1: Identify the value of the variables of the formula.

a1 = 5 d = 3 n = 10 an = ? Step 2: Substitute the values to the formula. Then, solve for an.

10 = 5+ (10- 1)3

= 5+ (9)3 = 5 +27

10 = 32

Therefore, the 10th term in the arithmetic sequence is 32. Example 3. In the arithmetic sequence: 3, 9, 15, 21, … ; find the value of n if an = 75. Solution: Step 1: Identify the value of the variables of the formula.

a1 = 3 d = 6 an = 75 n = ? Substitute the values to the formula. Then, solve for n. 75 = 3+ ( -1)6

-----

75 = 3+ 6 - 6 75 - 3+ 6 = 6 78 = 6 n = 13 Therefore, 75 is in the 13th term of the arithmetic sequence.5 Example 4. The 5th term of an arithmetic sequence is 20, and the 12th term is 48. Find the following: (a) common difference, (b) first term, and (c) the 30th term. Solution: Step 1: Identify the given values. a5 = 20 a12 = 485 Step 2: Substitute the given values to the formula For a5 = 20 20 = a1 + (5 - 1) d 20 = a1 + 4d (Equation 1) For a12 = 48 48 = a1 + (12 - 1) d 48 = a1 + 11d (Equation 2) Step 3: Subtract the equations 20 = a1 + 4d 48 = a1 + 11d

---

-28 = -7d

d = 4

Step 4: Substitute the value of d = 4 to equation 1: 20 = a1 + 4(4) 20 = a1 + 16 20 - 16 = a1 a1 = 4 Step 5. Solve for the 30th term. a30 = 4 + (30 - 1) 4

= 4 + (29)4 = 4 + 116 a30 = 1205 Therefore, the common difference is 4, the first term is 4, and the 30th term is 120.

Example 5. During the dry season, a farmer in your town is testing a new water irrigation system for his rice field. After 1 minute of turning on the water pump, the water level in the irrigation canal rose to 30 centimeters. After 2 minutes, it reached 85 centimeters, and after 3 minutes, the level was 140 centimeters. If the water level continues to rise at this constant rate, how high will the water level be after 16 minutes? Solution: Let an = the water level after n minutes Step 1: Identify the given.

a1 = 30cm d = 55 cm n = 16 min an = ? Step 2: Substitute the given values to the formula. a16 = 30 + (16 - 1)55

= 30 + (15) ∙ 55 = 30 + 825 a16 = 855 cm Therefore, after 16 minutes, the water level will be 855 centimeters high in the irrigation canal.

##### Arithmetic Series

*Arithmetic Series is the sum of terms of an arithmetic sequence. It* represents adding the terms of the sequence together. Let's say we have the arithmetic sequence: 3, 6, 9, 12. This is an arithmetic sequence where the common difference d = 3. Now, let's add the terms: 3 + 6 + 9 + 12 = 30. The sum is an example of arithmetic series. Let us compute the sum of the first terms of an arithmetic series.

= ++. . .+ = +( +)+ ( + )+. . .+[ + (- )]

There are terms, so we add together all the 1's

= 1 +[1+ 2+. . .+( - 1)]

We have already derived the formula for the sum of the numbers from 1 to n-1.

-----

= 1 + ( -1)/2 = [2 1 + ( - 1)]/2 Now we know that 1 +( -1) = . Therefore,

= (1 + )/2 We therefore have the following formulas to compute the sum of the first n terms of an arithmetic series: If the last nth term (an) is given: = (1 + ), or

2

If the last nth term (an) is not given:

= [2 1 + ( - 1)]

2

where: Sn = the sum of first n terms a1 = first term an = last nth term d = common difference Example 1. Find the sum of the arithmetic sequence with 10 terms: 3, 10, 17, …, 66. Solution: Since the last nth term is given, use the 1st formula. Step 1. Identify the given values. a1 = 3 an = 66 n = 10 Sn = ? Step 2. Substitute the values to the formula.

10 10 = (3 + 66) 2 = 5 (69)

10 = 345

Therefore, the sum of the arithmetic sequence is 345. Example 2: Find the sum of the first 15 terms of the arithmetic sequence: 4, 9, 14, 19, … Solution: Since the last nth term is not given, use the 2nd formula. Step 1. Identify the given values. a1 = 4 n = 15 d = 5 Sn = ?

-----

| Step 2. Substitute the given values to the formula. 12 = [2(4) + (15 - 1)5] = 7.5 [8 + (14)5] = 7.5 [8 + 70] = 7.5 [78] 12 = 585 Therefore, the sum of the arithmetic sequence is 585. Example 3. Mang Tonyo is a kalesa driver in the historic streets of Vigan, Ilocos Sur. On his first day, he earns ₱200. As more tourists visit throughout the week, his earnings increase by ₱100 each day. If this pattern continues for 15 days, how much will Mang Tonyo earn in total? Solution: Since the last earning or nth term is not given, use the 2nd formula. Let an = the earning of Mang Tonyo on day n. Step 1: Identify the given values. a1 = ₱200 d = ₱100 n = 15 days Sn=? Step 2: Substitute the given values to the formula. 15 15 = [2(200) + (15 - 1)100] 2 = 7.5 [400 + (14)100] = 7.5 [400 + 1400] = 7.5 [1800] 15 =₱13, 500 Therefore, Mang Tonyo will earn a total of ₱13,500 in 15 days. |  |

*Instructions:*

1. Upload enhancement problems into an interactive digital platform.
2. Present real-life contexts (desks, stairs, toothpicks) as structured digital tasks.
3. Instruct learners to solve individually or in pairs using guided input fields.
4. Require learners to identify key values (a₁, d, n) before solving.
5. Encourage step-by-step submission of solutions for better tracking of reasoning.
6. Provide instant feedback or hints where possible to support correction of errors.
7. Facilitate a short synthesis discussion comparing different solution approaches.
8. Highlight correct strategies and common misconceptions observed in responses.

**III. CONTENT Lesson 2.4. Geometric Sequence and Series**

At the end of the lesson, the learners shall be able to:

1. illustrate the geometric sequence;
2. define geometric sequences and series, including their key components;
3. calculate the nth term of a geometric sequence;

##### IV. OBJECTIVES

4. compute the geometric series;
5. solve problem involving geometric sequences and series; and
6. demonstrate appreciation of the importance of arithmetic sequences and series by explaining or presenting how they are used in making informed decisions in real-life situations.

##### Geometric Series

Review of terms:

1. First Term (a₁): This is the very first term of the sequence. It's the starting value from which all other terms are derived by multiplication with the common ratio.

-----

2. Common Ratio (r): This is the constant factor by which each term in the sequence is multiplied to get the next term. It is the ratio of all consecutive terms in the series.
4. Sₙ: This is the sum of the first n terms of a geometric sequence.

To derive the formula for the sum of the first n terms of a geometric sequence. A geometric sequence has: First term: a1 Common ratio: r Number of terms: n When r ≠ 1, let:

= 1 + 1 + 1 2 + 1 3 + ⋯ + 1 -1 eq. (1) Now, multiply both sides of equation (1) by r:

= 1 +1 2 +1 3 + ⋯ +1 eq. (2)

Next, subtract equation (2) from equation (1):

= 1 +1 + 1

= 1 + 1 2 + 1 3 + ⋯ + 1

---

Extract Sn as a common factor on the left-hand side of the equation and a1 on the right-hand side.

Now, divide both sides 1 - r.

This gives us a formula for a finite geometric series with What happens if the series is infinite? This means that very large. If we look at the expression on the right side of the equation, the part affected by -1), also becomes large and we don't get a real number value for . | alkansya (piggy bank). On the first day, he puts ₱20. Each following day, he doubles the amount he saves from the previous day. He plans to continue this for 7 days. How much total money will Mang Tonyo have saved in the alkansya after 7 days? **C. Demonstrating** ***C.1. Finding Practical Application***

| Knowledge and | Activity C.1. |
|---|---|
| Skills | Making Real-World Connections: Smart Budgeting Through Growth Patterns |

*Instructions:*

1. Assume you start with ₱10 mobile load on Day 1.
2. Each day, your load doubles for 5 days.
3. Complete a table showing the load amount for Day 1 to Day 5.
4. Use a geometric sequence to determine:
  a. The load amount on Day 5.
  b. The total load accumulated over 5 days.
5. Analyze the results and reflect on the sustainability of the pattern.
6. *Discuss your findings with a partner or group and compare* insights. *Scenario Summary* You are given ₱10 mobile load today. Each day, your load doubles to ensure enough data and communication needs for online learning and social media use. Your goal is to determine whether this habit is sustainable over a 5-day period. *Processing Questions:*
1. What pattern did you observe in the daily increase of your load?
2. How does doubling each day relate to a geometric sequence?
3. What is the significance of identifying the common ratio in this situation?
4. Was the total load over 5 days realistic or excessive? Why?
5. What real-life challenges could arise from exponential growth in spending?
6. How would you modify the pattern to make the load usage more sustainable?

Activity sheet or worksheet Pen or pencil Calculator (optional) Table for daily load tracking *Instructions:*

How much will Carlo save during the 6th month? *Solution:* a₁ = 2,000 d = 500 n = 6 aₙ = a₁ + (n-1) d a₆ = 2,000 + (6-1) (500) a₆ = 2,000 + 2,500 a₆ = ₱4,500 *Final Answer: Carlo will save ₱4,500 during the 6th month.* Example 2. Geometric Sequence in Investment Angela invests ₱2,000 in an account earning 5% annual interest compounded yearly.

Sequence: Compound Interest:

3 = -1 A = P(1 + )

1 3 = 2100(1.05) 3-1 A = 2,000(1.05)

*Final Answer: Angela's investment will grow to ₱2,315.25* after 3 years.

##### Key Concept Reminder:

- Savings with fixed increase → Arithmetic sequence (constant difference)
- Investment with compounding → Geometric sequence (constant ratio)

= 1 + ( - 1) Substitute the values:

1. What type of sequence is involved?
2. What is the common ratio?

##### Solution:

*Given:*

1 = 1 + 0.5 1 = 1.05

### = 3

0 = 10,000 1 = 10,000 1.05 1 = 10,500

Sequence: Compound Interest:

3 = -1 A = P(1+ )

1 3 = 10,500(1.05) 3-1 A = 10,000(1.05)3

3 = 11,576.25 A = 10,000(1.157625)

A = ₱11,576.25 *Final Answer:* Angela's investment will grow to ₱11,576.25 after 3 years.

Example 3. Periodic Loan Payment A family pays ₱15,000 monthly for a housing loan for 5 years. Guide Questions:

1. What financial situation is presented?
2. What mathematical patterns are involved?
