1 = 15,000

### d = 15,000 = 5x12

### = 60

Total payment:

Monthly Payment = ₱15,000 Number of Months = 5(12) = 605 Total Payment:

15,000 × 60 = ₱900,000 *Final Answer: The family will pay a total of ₱900,000 after 5 years.* Example 4. Accumulated Savings through Periodic Deposits A student deposits ₱2,000 every month into a savings account for 12 months without withdrawing money. The account does not yet include interest. *Guide Questions:*

1. What financial situation is presented?
2. What mathematical patterns are involved?
3. How much total money will the student accumulate after 12 months?

##### Solution:

Given:

1 = 2,000

### = 2,000 n = 12

-----

Use the arithmetic sequence formula:

= 1 + ( - 1)

Sequence: Total Accumulated Value = 12 = 2,000 + (12-1) (2,000) Monthly Deposit × Number of

12 = 2,000 + 22,000 Months

12 = ₱24,000 Total Amount = 2,000 × 12

Total Amount = ₱24,000

*Final Answer: The student will accumulate ₱24,000 after 12* months.

This situation shows repeated equal deposits over time. The

**accumulated value follows a series because the deposits are continuously added. This concept is commonly used in savings**

plans, investment programs, and retirement funds.

### Unit 3. CONVERSION OF MEASUREMEMNT

##### I. LEARNING GOALS

**Content** The learners demonstrate knowledge and understanding of measurement and conversion.

##### Standard

| Performance | By the end of the term, the learners are able to design and present a practical measurement or conversion solution with a concrete or |
|---|---|
| Standard | visual model. Contextualized Performance Standards: |

*By the end of the term, the learners are able to design and present a practical solution to a disaster-related problem in their respective* *barangay. Solution must include a concrete visual model, like a sketch, blueprint, layout, or diagram, and must show correct measurements,* *units, conversions, and scale.* **Learning** The learners… **Competencies** 1. determine and apply the appropriate metric units for various contexts such as units for small and large quantities and scientific

measurements.

2. convert measurements with different units in the same system and between different systems.
3. determine the surface area of three-dimensional objects such as prisms, pyramids, cylinders, cones, spheres and combinations of these.
4. calculate the volume of prisms, pyramids, cylinders, cones, spheres and combinations of these.
5. Use a scale to interpret and compare distances on maps, blueprints, and sketches.
6. solve problems involving conversion of currencies.
7. apply time conversions in various real-world scenarios such as travel planning, project management, and daily routine schedules for personal and business transactions.
8. apply temperature conversions to real-world scenarios such as recipe adjustments, weather reports, scientific experiments, storage conditions, and energy efficiency.

| B.2. Discussing the Concept |  |
| Activity B.2. Deepening Conceptual Understanding Part I. Metric Units and Metric Prefixes Metric units are standardized units of measurement used in science, mathematics, engineering, medicine, and daily life. The modern metric system used worldwide is called the International System of Units (SI). The SI system establishes standard base units for measuring different physical quantities such as length, mass, time, and temperature. The metric system is based on powers of 10, making measurement conversions easier and more systematic. The seven SI base units include: Quantity Common Metric Units Length centimeter (cm), kilometer (km), millimeter (mm) Mass gram (g), milligram (mg), metric ton (t) | Activity B.2. Deepening Conceptual Understanding Facilitation Guide: Part I. Metric Units & Prefixes 1. Discuss learners' prior experiences and identify SI base units. Processing Questions: a. Which of the SI base units are you most familiar with? Where do you commonly encounter these units in your daily life? b. Which of these units do you rarely encounter? Why do you think that is the case? c. Aside from the seven SI base units, what other metric units do you often encounter in your daily life? 2. Explore metric prefixes and their powers of ten. 3. Have learners complete Check Your Understanding collaboratively. ✔️Check Your Understanding! Instructions: Identify the most appropriate metric unit for each quantity. Justify your answer. a. Distance between two major cities b. Amount of syrup in a medicine cup c. Thickness of a single-strand hair d. Electrical power rating of a refrigerator e. Weight limit indicated on a bridge warning sign 4. Discuss answers immediately to clarify misconceptions. |

prefixes. Metric prefixes help describe quantities that are much smaller or much larger than the base unit. Let us examine the metric prefix scale.

![](img_p116_1.png)

Part II. Scientific Notation Metric prefixes help us describe quantities that are much smaller or much larger than a unit. For example, the thickness of a hair may be measured in micrometers, while the distance between cities may be measured in kilometers. However, some measurements are so small or so large that writing them using ordinary numbers can become lengthy and prone to errors. To express very small and very large numbers in a shorter and more manageable form, mathematicians and scientists use scientific notation.

##### Scientific notation is a mathematical method used to express

very large or very small numbers in a shorter and more manageable form. It is written as: × where:

is a coefficient greater than or equal to 1 but less than 10, and is an integer exponent indicating the power of 10.

##### Converting Standard Form to Scientific Notation

*Example 1.* A virus has a diameter of 0.00000006 meter. Express the measurement in scientific notation.

*Guide Questions:*

1. In 0.00000006, where is the decimal point and how should it be moved so that the resulting number is greater than or equal to 1 but less than 10?
2. How many places was the decimal point moved, and in what direction was it moved?
3. How is the number of decimal movements related to the exponent in scientific notation?
4. How can the number be written in scientific notation?
5. *What would be a more appropriate metric unit for expressing* the diameter of the virus? Explain your answer.

Appropriate metric unit: nanometer (nm), because the diameter of the virus is represented by 10⁻⁸ meter, which is close to the nanoprefix (10⁻⁹). Using nanometers makes the measurement easier to express as 60 nm. *Example 2.* A fully loaded truck has a mass of 12,000,000 grams. Express the mass in scientific notation. *Guide Questions:*

1. How was the decimal point moved so that the coefficient became greater than or equal to 1 but less than 10?
2. How many places was the decimal point moved?
3. What relationship do you observe between the decimal movement and the exponent?
4. How can the number be written in scientific notation?
5. What would be a more appropriate metric unit for expressing the mass of the loaded truck? Explain your answer.

##### Converting Scientific Notation to Standard Form

*Example 1.*

![](img_p118_3.png)

A scientific calculator displays the result of a computation as:

8.192 × 1013

Express the number in standard form. *Guide Questions:*

1. In 8.192 × 1013 , what does the exponent

indicate about the size of the quantity?

2. Where is the decimal point initially placed in the coefficient in 8.192?
3. How many places should the decimal point be moved, and in what direction should it be moved?
4. How is the exponent related to the movement of the decimal point?
5. How can the number be written in standard form?

![](img_p118_1.png)

*Example 2.* A laboratory report shows that a blood sample contains:

4.7 × 10-9 gram/mL of a certain hormone. Express the measurement in standard form. *Guide Questions:*

1. In 4.7 × 10-9 , what does the exponent indicate about the size of the quantity?
2. Where is the decimal point initially placed in the coefficient 4.7?
3. How many places should the decimal point be moved, and in what direction should it be moved?
4. How is the exponent related to the movement of the decimal point?
5. How can the number be written in standard form?

![](img_p118_2.png)

##### To convert scientific notation to standard form:

1. Identify the exponent.

-----

2. Move the decimal point to the right if the exponent is positive.
3. Move the decimal point to the left if the exponent is negative.
4. Write the resulting number. Part III. Exploring Scientific Notation Using a Scientific

##### Calculator

Scientific calculators can also be used to represent very large or very small numbers in scientific notation. It is traditionally represented in E notation, where E means "× 10 raised to the power of." For example:

0.00000006 = 6E-8 12,000,000 = 1.2E7 This is commonly used in spreadsheets and some digital calculators to represent very large or very small numbers. However, many modern scientific calculators display numbers using Natural Display, which shows scientific notation using ×10 rather than E. For example:

0.00000006 may appear as 6 × 10⁻⁸ 12,000,000 may appear as 1.2 × 10⁷ Both formats represent the same values. Understanding E notation helps learners interpret spreadsheet outputs, older digital displays, and other computational devices, while Natural Display shows how many modern scientific calculators present scientific notation in a format that closely resembles standard mathematical notation.

Watch the video tutorial and observe how the calculator displays numbers in scientific notation using the link and QR provided.

![](img_p119_1.png)

##### C.1. Finding Practical Application C.

**Demonstrating** Activity C.1. Making Real-World Connections Activity C.1. Making Real-World Connections

##### Knowledge and Skills Practical Applications of Scientific Measurement

*Instructions:* Read and analyze each scenario and answer the questions. *Practical Application 1.* individually or in pairs.

##### Facilitation Guide:

1. Present the practical application scenarios through printed materials, slides, or a digital display.
2. Ask learners to analyze each situation and answer the questions

-----

| A barangay is planning a rainwater harvesting system to help address water shortages during periods of drought. The proposed storage tank can hold 15,000 liters of water. If this capacity will be included in the design specifications, would it be more appropriate to express it as 15,000 L or 15 kl? Explain your answer. Practical Application 2. During a La Niña season, a farmer uses a filtration system to help manage excess water in an irrigation channel and protect agricultural crops from flooding. The filter contains pores with an average diameter of 0.000002 meter. If this measurement is included in the technical specifications of the filtration system, how can it be expressed more efficiently? Write the measurement in scientific notation and identify a more appropriate metric unit that may be used. Explain your answer. | 3. Encourage learners to justify their answers using concepts of appropriate metric units and scientific notation. 4. Facilitate a class discussion and allow selected learners to explain their reasoning. 5. Guide learners in evaluating how the choice of units and numerical representations affects the clarity, accuracy, and practicality of communicating measurements. 6. Emphasize that appropriate units and scientific notation help make measurements easier to interpret and apply in real-world situations. Processing Questions 1. How did the context of each situation help you decide on the most appropriate unit or representation of the measurement? 2. Why is it important to communicate measurements using units and formats that are easy for the intended audience to understand? 3. How do metric prefixes and scientific notation help make very large or very small measurements easier to interpret and communicate? |

##### Metric System vs. English System

The previous activity showed that some measurements were easier to compare than others. The difficulty arose because the measurements belonged to two different systems of measurement.

The Metric System is a standardized system of measurement based on powers of ten. It is widely used around the world, including in the Philippines. Common metric units include meter, kilometer, centimeter, gram, kilogram, liter, milliliter, and degree Celsius. The English System of Measurement is another system used in some countries, particularly the United States. Common English units include inch, foot, yard, mile, ounce, pound, gallon, quart, and degree Fahrenheit. The Metric System uses a base-10 structure, making conversions systematic. In contrast, the English System uses varying conversion factors between units. Because measurements may be expressed using different systems, conversion is necessary to compare and communicate measurements accurately.

-----

| B.2. Discussing the Concept |  |
|---|---|
| Activity B.2. Deepening Conceptual Understanding Part I. Converting Metric Units The Metric System is based on powers of ten. This means that units are related by multiples or fractions of ten, making conversions systematic and consistent. Recall the metric prefix scale: The metric prefix scale can be used to convert measurements within the Metric System. Since the Metric System is based on powers of ten, each prefix represents a value that is ten times larger or ten times smaller than the adjacent prefix. Example. Convert 4.5 kilometers to centimeters. Solution: Given unit: kilometers; desired unit: centimeters Conversions: 1 km = 103m; 1 cm = 10-2m Part II. Converting English Units The English System uses conversion relationships that do not follow powers of ten. As a result, conversion equivalences must be identified before appropriate conversion factors can be written. | Activity B.2. Deepening Conceptual Understanding This activity consists of three parts: • Part I: Converting Metric Units • Part II: Converting English Units • Part III: Converting Between Metric and English Systems, including temperature Part I. Converting Metric Units Facilitation Guide: 1. Display the Metric Prefix Scale for reference. 2. Ask guide questions progressively: given unit → desired unit → first conversion factor → subsequent factors. Guide Questions: a. What is the given unit? b. What is the desired unit? c. Using the metric prefix scale, what conversion can be written to relate the given unit to the desired unit? d. What conversion factor can be used to cancel the given unit kilometer? Why is the first conversion factor written as 103m and not 1km e. ? 1km 103m f. What conversion factor can be used to cancel the remaining unit meter? g. After all cancellations, what unit remains? h. Why does multiplying by conversion factors not change the actual measurement? 3. Emphasize that multiplying by a conversion factor = multiplying by 1; units change but quantity remains. 4. Encourage learners to identify given and desired units before applying conversion factors. 5. Highlight unit cancellation: given unit disappears, desired unit remains. 6. After worked examples, learners attempt Check Your Understanding problems step by step. ✔️Check Your Understanding! Convert each measurement to the indicated unit. Show the complete solution using conversion factors. a. Convert 7.2 kilometers to millimeters. b. Convert 450 milligrams to kilograms. c. Convert 0.85 megameters to centimeters. d. Convert 3.5 milliliters to kiloliters. |

Part II. Converting English Units *Facilitation Guide:*

1. Show the English conversion table (length, mass, capacity).
2. Guide learners to identify given and desired units first. Guide Questions:
    a. What is the given unit?
    b. What is the desired unit?
    c. Which conversion factor cancels gallons?
    d. After applying the first conversion factor, what unit remains?
    e. Which conversion factor cancels quarts?
    f. Which conversion factor cancels pints?
    g. After all cancellations, what unit remains?
    h. Why is it important to follow the correct sequence of conversion factors when converting between multiple units?
3. Emphasize correct sequence of conversion factors to cancel units properly.
4. Reinforce that each factor = 1, only units change.
5. Monitor common errors: reversed numerator/denominator, skipped steps, wrong sequencing. Example: Convert 2.5 gallons to cups 6. Provide Check Your Understanding exercises for independent *Solution:* practice. Part III. Converting Between the Metric and English Systems Measurements are not always expressed using the same system of units. In many situations, measurements given in the Metric System must be converted to the English System, or vice versa. To perform these conversions, appropriate conversion equivalences are used to form conversion factors. These conversion factors allow the given unit

**C. Digital Tool Spotlight: Verifying Conversions with a Unit**

| Demonstrating | Converter |
|---|---|
| Knowledge and | The previous activity required the manual application of unit |
| Skills | conversion skills across a variety of situations. In many real-world settings, digital tools are also used to verify measurements and |

reduce computational errors when making decisions.

One example is a unit converter, which is available on many smartphones, calculators, and websites. A unit converter can quickly convert measurements from one unit to another and serve as a tool for checking the accuracy of manual computations.

Many smartphones available include a built-in unit converter, often integrated into the calculator application. When available, this feature may be used to verify conversion results. Online tools, such as

##### UnitConverters.net (https://www.unitconverters.net/), may also be

used as an alternative.

Before using a unit converter, it is important to identify:

- the quantity being measured (e.g., length, mass, volume, temperature, time);
- the given measurement value and unit; and
- the desired unit.

*Facilitation Guide:*

1. Demonstrate the use of a unit converter before presenting the scenarios.
2. Emphasize that manual calculations must be completed before using digital verification tools.
3. Guide learners in identifying the quantity, given measurement value and unit, and desired unit before using the converter.
4. Allow the use of calculators, conversion tables, and unit converters for verification.
5. Require learners to show complete solutions and justify decisions using converted measurements.
6. Facilitate discussion on how conversion accuracy affects decision-making in real-life situations.

### 7. Accept minor rounding differences resulting from different

conversion tools.

*Processing Questions:*

1. How did converting units help you complete the tasks in each scenario?
2. What problems could occur if measurements are not converted accurately in cooking, production, or construction?
3. Why is it important to be able to convert between different systems of measurement in real-life situations?

*Example: Convert 6 feet to meters.* Using the Unit Converter

1. Identify the quantity to convert (e.g., Length, Temperature, Area, Volume, Weight, or Time).
2. Select the given unit from the drop-down menu on the left.
3. Select the desired unit from the drop-down menu on the right.
4. Enter the measurement value for the given unit in the input box.
5. The converter will automatically display the converted value in the desired unit. Now, apply the same process in the following real-world scenarios.
1. Complete the manual calculations first for each scenario.
2. Afterward, use the unit converter (phone-based or online, e.g., UnitConverters.net) to verify your results.
3. Compare the digital results with your manual computations and note any differences.

-----

4. Ensure all measurements, units, and reasoning are clearly recorded.

##### Scenario 1. Cooking from an International Recipe

A French recipe requires 1.2 liters of vegetable broth. You only have measuring cups at home.

##### Tasks:

1. Convert 1.2 liters into cups.
2. Determine whether 5 cups is enough to prepare the recipe.
3. Explain how accurate conversions help avoid mistakes in cooking.

##### Scenario 2. Dress Production

Maria's garment workshop received an order for 200 dresses. Each dress requires 2.5 yards of fabric. Her supplier sells fabric in meters. Maria wants to add 10% extra fabric to account for cutting waste.

##### Tasks:

1. Calculate the total fabric needed in yards.
2. Convert this amount to meters.
3. Include the 10% extra allowance.
4. Recommend how many meters Maria should order and explain why this matters for her production.

##### Scenario 3. Carpentry Measurement Challenge

A carpenter is building a small bookshelf. The design specifies the board length as 6 feet, but the boards are sold in meters at the hardware store.

##### Tasks:

1. Convert 6 feet into meters.
2. Determine the minimum board length he should buy.
3. Explain why accurate conversion is important when purchasing materials and planning a construction project.

##### Conversion Quest

1. Present stations one at a time and do not allow progression until all problems are correct.
2. Encourage learners to show stepwise calculations.
3. Use hints to scaffold learners who struggle.
4. Collect and verify letter clues before moving on.
5. Celebrate completion of the hidden word to motivate learners.

-----

##### Unit Conversion in Real Life

1. Encourage learners to select scenarios relevant to their interests or strand.
2. Ensure students demonstrate stepwise calculations and correct units.
3. Highlight reasoning and consequences of errors to reinforce the importance of accurate conversions.
4. Presenting to the class promotes communication skills and peer learning.

##### ANNOTATION FOR TEACHERS

Activity A.1. Leveling Learners Readiness:Warm-Up Discussion This activity is intended to activate learners' prior knowledge of twodimensional and three-dimensional geometry in preparation for the lesson on determining the surface area of solid figures. Through the review of area concepts and the identification of real-life threedimensional objects, learners are provided opportunities to recall prerequisite mathematical knowledge and connect previously learned concepts to the current lesson. The activity supports conceptual understanding by helping learners recognize the relationship between the area of two-dimensional figures and the total outer surface of three-dimensional objects. To facilitate this activity, the teacher should organize collaborative learning opportunities, provide appropriate visual references or concrete models, and guide learners in identifying the geometric properties of solid figures, including faces, edges, vertices, flat surfaces, and curved surfaces. The teacher should monitor learner participation, address misconceptions promptly, and encourage accurate mathematical communication during discussions and group tasks. Emphasis should be placed on relating learners' observations to the mathematical concepts required for determining surface area. Activity A.2.

**Appreciating Lesson Relevance: Cover It Up! Why Surface Area Matters**

Understanding the surface area of three-dimensional objects is important in many real-life situations involving design, packaging, construction, and manufacturing. In this activity, you will examine practical examples where determining the total outer covering of an object is necessary. *Instructions:*

3. Complete the observation table by identifying the geometric shape represented and its possible real-life use. Use your observations to think about the parts or surfaces that may need to be measured and why determining the surface area of the object may be important.

Activity A.2: Appreciating Learning Relevance This activity establishes the relevance of the lesson by helping learners connect the concept of surface area to practical and professional contexts where understanding the structure of threedimensional objects is essential. Through the analysis of blueprints, nets, and real-life object representations, learners develop spatial reasoning and recognize that identifying the faces and surfaces of solid figures is necessary before determining their total outer covering. This helps learners appreciate the practical application of geometry in fields such as architecture, engineering, construction, packaging, and product design. To facilitate this activity, the teacher should provide clear visual representations of blueprints, nets, or real-life objects and model how to analyze one example before learners begin the task. Learners should be guided to examine the shapes, identify structural features, and relate these to corresponding three-dimensional figures and reallife applications. The teacher should monitor learner participation, encourage collaborative discussion, address misconceptions, and emphasize the connection between recognizing object structure and selecting appropriate mathematical strategies for surface area.

Activity B.1.

**Exploring Key Concepts: Surface Area Escape Challenge**

This activity introduces learners to the process of determining the surface area of three-dimensional solid figures through guided examples presented in an engaging maze format. By examining the given formulas and substitutions, then completing the missing solution steps, learners develop an understanding of how appropriate surface area formulas are selected and applied to different solid figures such as cubes, cylinders, cones, rectangular prisms, and spheres. The guided structure provides scaffolded support that helps learners focus on understanding the computation process while building confidence in solving surface area problems. To facilitate this activity, the teacher should briefly review the surface area formulas included in the Formula Reference Card and model one sample problem before learners begin the maze. Learners may work in pairs or small groups to encourage discussion, collaborative reasoning, and peer support. As learners progress through the activity, the teacher should monitor participation, provide immediate feedback, and clarify misconceptions related to formula selection, substitution, and computation. Emphasis should be placed on helping learners recognize the relationship between the dimensions of a solid figure and the corresponding formula used to determine its surface area. ##### Understanding Surface Area of Three-Dimensional Objects

Surface area is the total area of all the outer faces and surfaces of a three-dimensional object. Determining surface area helps estimate the amount of material needed to cover, package, construct, or manufacture an object. To determine the surface area of a solid figure, it is important to identify its faces or surfaces and the measurements needed to find their areas. Since solid figures have different characteristics, each has a corresponding surface area formula. Imagine unfolding a solid figure into a flat pattern called a net. Each part of the net represents a face or surface of the solid. By finding the area of each face and adding them together, we obtain the total surface area of the object. Surface area formulas are simply shortcuts that help us add the areas of all faces efficiently.

##### A. Rectangular Prism

A rectangular prism has six rectangular faces. Opposite faces are congruent, and the total surface area is the sum of the areas of all six faces. Formula: = 2 + 2ℎ + 2ℎ Measurements Needed: length (l), width (w), height (h) Example: A packaging engineer is developing a protective container for transporting laboratory equipment. The container measures 30 cm long, 20 cm wide, and 10 cm high. How much packaging material is needed to construct the container? Solution: Given: l = 30 cm, w = 20 cm, h = 10 cm

= 2(30)(20) + 2(30)(10) + 2(20)(10)

Activity B.2. Deepening Conceptual Understanding

This activity develops learners' understanding of the surface area of common three-dimensional figures by examining their faces or surfaces, identifying the measurements needed, and applying the appropriate formulas. Contextualized examples from engineering, manufacturing, agriculture, and architecture help learners relate mathematical concepts to real-world applications. Through guided examples and independent practice, learners strengthen their ability to determine the surface area of different three-dimensional objects, which directly supports the learning competency.

Digital presentation tools, interactive simulations, or 3D modeling applications (e.g., GeoGebra 3D, PowerPoint 3D Models, or online geometry visualizers) may be used to help learners visualize the faces, surfaces, and dimensions of solid figures and explore how changes in measurements affect surface area.

Determine the amount of material needed to cover one storage unit. Solution: Given: s = 12 cm = 6(12²) = 6(144) = 864 ² The storage unit requires 864 cm² of covering material. C. Cylinder A cylinder has two circular bases and one curved surface. Its surface area includes the areas of both circular bases and the curved surface. Formula: = 2² + 2ℎ Measurements Needed: radius (r), height (h) Example: A municipal water district plans to install a cylindrical storage tank with a radius of 3 m and a height of 7 m. Determine the amount of metal sheet required to construct the tank. Solution: Given: r = 3 m, h = 7 m = 2(3²) + 2(3)(7) = 18 + 42 = 60 ≈ 188.50 ² Approximately 188.50 m² of metal sheet is needed. D. Cone A cone has one circular base and one curved surface. The slant height is used to determine the area of the curved surface. Formula: = ² + | Processing Questions: 1. Which solid figures have both flat and curved surfaces? Which solid figure has only a curved surface? 4. Why is slant height used in finding the surface area of a cone and a square pyramid? 5. How does identifying the characteristics of a solid figure help in selecting the appropriate surface area formula? 6. How can surface area calculations be useful in packaging, manufacturing, engineering, and construction? 7. Why is it important to determine the surface area of an object before constructing, packaging, or covering it? |

-----

Measurements Needed: radius (r), slant height (s) Example: An agricultural facility designs a conical hopper used to transfer grain into storage bins. The hopper has a radius of 0.5 m and a slant height of 1.3 m. Determine the amount of metal sheet needed to construct the hopper. Solution: Given: r = 0.5 m, s = 1.3 m

= (0.5²) + (0.5)(1.3) = 0.25 + 0.65 = 0.90 ≈ 2.83 ² Approximately 2.83 m² of metal sheet is needed to construct the hopper.

##### E. Sphere

A sphere has one continuous curved surface and no flat faces. Its surface area depends only on its radius. Formula: = 4² Measurement Needed: radius (r) Example: An engineering firm designs a spherical storage vessel with a radius of 1.2 m for a laboratory prototype. Determine the surface area of the vessel. Solution: Given: r = 1.2 m

SA = 4π(1.2²) SA = 5.76π SA ≈ 18.10 m² The spherical storage vessel has a surface area of approximately 18.10 m². F. Square Pyramid A square pyramid has one square base and four triangular faces. Its surface area is the sum of the area of the base and the areas of the triangular faces. Formula: = ² + 2 Measurements Needed: base edge length (b), slant height (s) Example: An architectural firm designs a pyramid-shaped roof for a gazebo in a commercial park. The roof has a square base measuring 8 m on each side and a slant height of 6 m. Determine the amount of roofing material needed to cover the gazebo roof.

-----

| Solution: Given: b = 8 m, s = 6 m = 8² + 2(8)(6) = 64 + 96 = 160 ² The gazebo roof requires 160 m² of glass. **Making Real-World Connections: Surface Area Planning Challenge**

This activity provides learners with an opportunity to apply their understanding of surface area in a practical and meaningful context. By estimating the amount of construction material needed for the different components of a proposed community information center, learners connect mathematical concepts to authentic situations encountered in architecture, engineering, and construction. The activity develops learners' abilities to identify the dimensions of threedimensional figures, select and apply appropriate surface area formulas, perform accurate computations, and interpret results to support planning and decision-making. These activities provide opportunities for learners to strengthen or extend their understanding of surface area based on their learning needs. The remediation activities focus on reinforcing prerequisite concepts, formula application, visualization of solid figures, and connections to real-life situations. The enhancement activities challenge learners to apply surface area concepts in more complex, open-ended, and authentic tasks involving design, modeling, and problem solving. Through these activities, learners continue to develop mathematical reasoning, creativity, and the ability to apply surface area concepts in meaningful contexts. Interactive geometry applications, virtual manipulatives, GeoGebra 3D, digital drawing tools, presentation software, and collaborative online platforms may be used to visualize solid figures, create designs, explore composite structures, and present outputs. **III. CONTENT Lesson 3.4. Calculating the Volume of Prisms, Pyramids, Cylinders, Cones, Spheres and Combinations of These.**

By the end of the lesson, learners will be able to:

**IV.**

1. identify the required measurements (e.g., base area, height, radius, and diameter) needed to determine the volume of a given solid

figure;

2. select and apply the appropriate volume formula for prisms, pyramids, cylinders, cones, and spheres; and
3. solve routine, contextualized, and real-life problems involving the volume of individual and composite solid figures, and express answers using the appropriate cubic units.

Appreciating Learning Relevance: Volume in Real Life This activity helps learners appreciate the relevance of volume through differentiated real-life scenarios involving storage, transportation, and design. By engaging learners in situations of varying complexity, the activity accommodates different readiness levels while guiding all learners toward the same understanding of volume as a practical and useful mathematical concept. Before the activity, prepare and distribute the Activity Cards (Refer to the Teacher's Notes) according to learners' readiness levels. Groups may be assigned scenarios based on learners' readiness levels or selected randomly. Option 1 provides guided support through a familiar household context, Option 2 focuses on practical applications in packaging and transportation, and Option 3 offers a more complex problem involving planning and design. Allow groups sufficient time to discuss the scenario and complete the Activity Sheet. As learners share their responses, facilitate a discussion highlighting the common need to determine the amount of space an object occupies, or a container can hold. Encourage learners to connect the scenarios to their own experiences and future careers. To enhance |

Before the activity, prepare station cards containing the problem situations and corresponding illustrations of the solid figures. Organize learners into small groups and assign a fixed amount of time for each station. Encourage learners to identify the given measurements, select the appropriate formula, perform accurate computations, and express answers using correct units of measure. As learners rotate through the stations, monitor participation, provide immediate feedback, and address misconceptions related to formula selection, substitution, and computation. After the activity, facilitate a discussion of the solutions and processing questions. Encourage learners to explain the strategies they used and compare different approaches to solving the problems. Activity C.1.

**Making Real-World Connections: Design a Mini Storage Box**

This activity provides learners with an opportunity to apply volume formulas in a practical and familiar context. By designing a mini storage box, learners use mathematical reasoning to select an appropriate solid figure, determine dimensions, calculate volume, and justify design decisions. The task encourages learners to connect mathematical concepts with everyday applications involving storage and packaging. Before the activity, discuss common storage containers used at home, in school, and in workplaces. Encourage learners to consider the purpose of their design and the amount of space needed to store specific items. As groups work, monitor participation, guide learners in selecting appropriate formulas, and provide feedback on their calculations and designs. After the presentations, facilitate a discussion on how volume calculations are used in fields such as packaging, product design, warehousing, manufacturing, and logistics. To enhance engagement and visualization, digital tools such as Canva, GeoGebra, Google Slides, PowerPoint, or simple 3D design applications may be used to create and present the storage box designs. This activity enables learners to apply their understanding of volume to solve authentic real-world problems.

Activity C.2. Wrapping Up the Lesson This activity provides learners with an opportunity to synthesize and generalize the concepts learned about the volume of prisms, cylinders, pyramids, cones, and spheres. Through collaborative discussion and written generalization, learners identify relationships among volume formulas, recognize common patterns, and formulate key principles used in solving volume-related problems. For Option 1, facilitate group discussions and guide learners in identifying similarities, differences, and recurring patterns among the formulas. Encourage groups to prepare concise and accurate Generalization Posters that summarize the essential concepts, formulas, and applications discussed throughout the lesson. For Option 2, require learners to construct a written generalization that demonstrates their understanding of volume concepts and their applications. During the synthesis phase, consolidate learners' responses and emphasize that volume measures the amount of space occupied by a three-dimensional figure. Reinforce the importance of selecting the appropriate formula and measurements when solving volume problems and highlight the practical applications of volume in various fields. To support collaboration and presentation, digital tools such as Canva, Padlet, Google Slides, PowerPoint, or Jamboard may be utilized. This activity serves as the culmination of the lesson by helping learners organize, connect, and generalize their understanding of volume concepts before the assessment phase.

***C.3. Evaluating Learning (Please see the Learning Activity Sheet for learners instructions on this activity.)***

Activity C.3. Assessing Learning Outcomes

Facilitate the activity in the LAS. To enhance learner engagement, you may use the suggested strategy. This assessment evaluates learners' attainment of the lesson objectives by measuring their ability to identify appropriate volume formulas, perform accurate computations, and apply volume concepts in real-life situations. The assessment progresses from formula recognition to procedural calculations and culminates in contextualized application problems involving solid figures and composite solids. Before administering the assessment, remind learners to identify the given measurements, select the appropriate formula, and express answers using correct units. During the assessment, monitor learners' work and encourage them to show complete solutions. After checking the outputs, discuss common misconceptions related to formula selection, substitution, and computation. Assessment results may be used to identify learners who require remediation or enrichment activities. Digital platforms such as Google Forms, Microsoft Forms, Quizizz, or Kahoot! may be utilized to administer the assessment and provide immediate feedback.

##### C.4. Additional Activities

Activity C.4. Extending and Reinforcing Learning

Facilitate the activity in the LAS. To enhance learner engagement, you may use any of the suggested strategies. This activity provides differentiated learning opportunities that address the varying needs of learners. The remediation tasks reinforce the identification of solid figures, selection of appropriate volume formulas, and accurate computation through guided and independent practice. These activities help learners strengthen foundational skills and build confidence in solving volume-related problems. The enrichment tasks extend learning by requiring learners to solve multistep and real-world problems involving storage, capacity, agriculture, and composite solids. These activities encourage mathematical reasoning, problem-solving, and the application of volume concepts in authentic contexts. The Design Challenge further develops creativity and decision-making through mathematical modeling. These activities may be assigned as classwork, homework, or supplementary learning tasks. Remediation should be provided to learners who need additional support in achieving mastery, while enrichment opportunities should be offered to learners who are ready to deepen and extend their understanding of volume concepts. For Design Challenge use the Rubric in the Teacher's Notes

Activity A.1. Leveling Learner Readiness *Facilitation Guide:*

1. Provide students with the original set of geometric figures and a set of candidate figures.
2. Explain the task: identify which figures are scaled copies without yet using the term scale.
3. Allow students to work individually or in pairs to drag, circle, or match the figures.
4. Encourage students to compare corresponding sides and note proportionality.
5. Ask students to explain the characteristics that helped them decide.
6. Facilitate a class discussion to summarize patterns and introduce the term scale after observations.
7. Guide students to make connections to real-world applications such as maps, blueprints, or sketches. *Processing Questions*

2. How does keeping parts proportional help make a representation accurate?
3. Why is it important for maps, blueprints, sketches, and layouts to maintain correct proportions?

| Activity B.2. Deepening Conceptual Understanding Part I. Understanding Proportions and Scaled Drawings A scale drawing is a drawing of an object or place that is larger or smaller than the actual object but keeps the same proportions. Maps, blueprints, floor plans, and sketches are common examples of scale drawings. For a drawing to be considered a scale drawing: • Proportionality: All parts must remain proportional. If one length is enlarged or reduced, all other lengths must be enlarged or reduced by the same amount. • Relative Position: Objects must keep their relative sizes and positions. For example, if a road is longer than another road in reality, it should also appear longer in the drawing. • Correspondence: Every part of the drawing must correspond to a real object or feature being represented. Part II. Reading and Interpreting Scales A scale tells us about the relationship between a measurement on a map, blueprint, sketch, or drawing, and the corresponding measurement in the real world. It allows large objects or distances to be represented in a manageable size while maintaining their proportions. Scales are commonly represented in three ways: 1. Unit Scale. A unit scale compares two different units of measurement. Example: 1 cm = 100 m This means that every 1 centimeter measured on the map represents 100 meters in the real world. 2. Ratio Scale. A ratio scale compares measurements using the same unit. Example: 1: 10,000 This means that 1 unit in the drawing represents 10,000 of the same units in reality. For example, 1 cm on the map represents 10,000 cm in the real world. 3. Graphic Scale (Scale Bar). A graphic scale is a labeled line segment or bar drawn directly on the map or blueprint | Activity B.2. Deepening Conceptual Understanding Facilitation Guide: 1. Present the activity in four parts: Part I: Proportions and recognizing scaled drawings Part II: Reading and interpreting scales Part III: Applying scales to measure and compare distances Part IV: Determining the scale factor 2. For each part, guide students through examples and mini- exercises, prompting them to explain reasoning. Check Your Understanding Part I. c. Why is a map considered a scale drawing? d. Can a drawing still be considered a scale drawing if some parts are enlarged while others are not? Explain. e. Why is maintaining proportionality important in maps, blueprints, and sketches? Part III. A route measures 4 cm on the map and the scale is 1 cm = 100 m. What is the actual distance? Two routes are measured on a map: Route A = 5 cm, Route B = 8 cm. The scale is 1 cm = 100 m. a. What are the actual distances of both routes? b. How much distance is saved by choosing the shorter route? f. Route A is shorter but passes through a flooded area, while Route B is longer but safer. Which route would you recommend? Explain your reasoning. Part IV a. A classroom measures 4 cm on a floor plan and 8 m in reality. Determine the scale factor. b. A garden measures 5 cm on a sketch and 25 m in reality. Determine the scale factor. |

*Example:* Learners can measure with a ruler and use the graphic scale to estimate real distances, reducing the need for complex calculations. Part III. Applying Scale to Measure and Compare Distances

1. Multiplication for conversion:
    - If you know the scale (e.g., 1 cm = 100 m), multiply the measured distance on the map by the scale factor to get the actual distance.

100

- *Example: 5* × = 500

1

2. Proportional reasoning:
    - For ratios (e.g., 1:10,000), multiply the map distance by the ratio to get the real-world distance. *Example: 4 cm × 10000* = 40,000 cm = 400 m

1

3. Using a ruler for direct measurement:
    - For a graphic scale, measure the distance between two points on the map using a ruler.
    - Compare the measured distance with the scale bar to estimate the corresponding real-world distance.

![](img_p162_2.png)

Part IV. Determining the Scale Factor Sometimes the scale of a drawing is not provided directly. In these situations, the scale factor can be determined by comparing a measurement on the drawing with its corresponding actual measurement. The scale factor is the number by which measurements in the drawing are multiplied to obtain the actual measurements.

##### Formula:

Actual Distance Scale Factor =

Drawing Distance c. A rectangular playground measures 6 cm by 4 cm in a

drawing and 30 m by 20 m in reality. Determine the scale factor and explain how you know the drawing is proportional.

3. Encourage students to measure map segments, calculate distances, and analyze real-world implications (e.g., route selection).
4. Use processing questions after each part to deepen understanding and check for HOTS thinking.

##### Processing Questions:

*Part II.*

a. How might the choice of scale (unit, ratio, or graphic) affect the accuracy and clarity of a map or blueprint?
b. If two maps of the same area use different scales, how would that change the way you interpret distances or compare routes?
c. What could happen if a scale is misread or misapplied when planning an evacuation route or construction project? Part III.
a. How can you calculate the scale factor when you know a distance on the drawing and the corresponding actual distance?
b. Why is determining the scale factor important before measuring or creating scaled sketches and layouts?
c. How can errors in calculating the scale factor affect measurements and proportionality in a map, blueprint, or design?
d. In what ways can knowing the scale factor help you compare different drawings or plan a real-world project effectively? Part IV.
a. Why is it necessary to express the drawing measurement and the actual measurement in the same unit before determining the scale factor?
b. How can the scale factor help you create an accurate scaled sketch, blueprint, or layout?
c. What problems might arise if the scale factor is determined incorrectly?
d. In what situations might you need to determine the scale factor instead of using a scale that is already given?
5. Provide feedback on calculations, proportional reasoning, and decision-making.

1. Work in small groups (3-5 learners).

6. Construct a scaled sketch, blueprint, or layout of your proposed design using either:
    a. digital tools such as Microsoft PowerPoint, Google Slides, Canva, Google Drawings, GeoGebra, or similar applications; or

##### Step-by-Step Instructions for Digital Tools Tools: Canva, Google Drawings, PowerPoint, GeoGebra

1. Open the application and create a new blank canvas or slide.
2. Insert shapes (rectangles, circles, polygons) torepresent compartments, plots, or features.
3. Use lines or arrows to show paths, routes, or movement.
4. Add text boxes for labeling dimensions, features, or scale.
5. Apply the chosen scale: measure real-world lengths and convert to drawing units.
6. Adjust shapes to reflect scaled distances accurately.

-----

7. Save or export your final scaled sketch, blueprint, or layout.

##### Step-by-Step Instructions for Manual Drawing Tools: Graphing paper, rulers, pencils, colored pencils

1. Choose graphing or bond paper to work on.
2. Draw the outline of your object or layout using a ruler.
3. Apply the chosen scale to convert real-world dimensions into drawing units.
4. Draw compartments, plots, or features to scale.
5. Label all measurements and features clearly.
6. Add any additional details (paths, access routes, or safe zones).
7. Review to ensure all components are proportional and readable.

Facilitate the activity in the LAS. Strictly follow the strictly follow the strategy provided.

##### Facilitation Guide

1. Organize learners according to their assigned IPT groups.
2. Ensure that learners have access to their Lesson 4.1 Key Measurements and Design Specifications and the materials needed for the activity.
3. Facilitate recall of the approved disaster-related innovation scenarios and clarify the assessment requirements.
4. Guide learners in reviewing their Lesson 4.1 outputs and Activity C.2 reflections, evaluated strengths and weaknesses, and generalizations.
5. Assist learners in identifying refinements to measurements and design ideas, ensuring improvements are based on proportional reasoning, not arbitrary placements.
6. Guide learners in selecting an appropriate scale that maintains proportionalities for all features in their sketch, blueprint, or layout.
7. Instruct learners to construct an initial scaled sketch, blueprint, or layout of their proposed innovation, applying proportional measurements consistently across all features.
8. Ensure learners clearly label all major features, measurements, dimensions, and the scale used.
9. Guide learners in converting selected scaled measurements to actual dimensions and comparing them to check for proportional accuracy and design feasibility.
10. Have learners write a brief design justification explaining how scale, measurements, and reflections informed their design decisions while maintaining proportionality.
11. Collect the completed visual models with scaling for assessment.
12. Allow groups to present their initial prototype designs and facilitate a brief discussion highlighting proportionality, accuracy, and design

reasoning.

##### Expected Output

1. Initial scaled sketch, blueprint, or layout of the proposed innovation
2. Clearly labeled scale, measurements, dimensions, and major features/components
3. Measurement conversions from scaled measurements to actual dimensions
4. Brief design justification explaining design decisions and application of scale
5. Completed Section IV. Visual Model with Scaling of Appendix B - Prototype Development Plan

##### C.4. Additional Activities

Activity C.4. Extending and Reinforcing Learning *Facilitation Guide:*

##### Remediation - Guided Prototype Design with Scaling

1. Identify learners who struggled in C.3 and provide targeted support.
2. Guide them to focus on one feature/component first to ensure proportionality and correct scale before revising the full design.
3. Provide scaffolding and demonstrations for scale, measurement, and labeling as needed (either manual or digital).
4. Check learners' proportionality and scale application before allowing them to continue to the remaining features.
5. Offer feedback and reinforcement throughout the process, focusing on accuracy, proportionality, and clarity.

-----

*Expected Output* Remediation - Guided Prototype Design with Scaling:

- Corrected scaled sketch, blueprint, or layout (focusing initially on one component)
- Labeled dimensions, features, and scale
- Demonstrated proportionality for the redrawn feature

##### Enrichment - Prototype Adaptability Plan

1. Group proficient learners and guide them in reviewing their initial prototype.
2. Facilitate discussions on adaptation scenarios (scale up/down, materials, budget, space, users, or environmental conditions).
3. Support learners in applying scale, proportionality, and measurement when modifying selected features.
4. Encourage learners to justify modifications and reflect on functionality, practicality, and safety.
5. Observe group discussions, provide formative feedback, and guide learners to think critically about design adaptability. *Expected Output* Enrichment - Prototype Adaptability Plan:
    - Revised scaled sketch, blueprint, or layout reflecting modifications for scaling up or down
    - Labeled updated features, dimensions, and scale
    - Computations showing how the scale was applied to adjust measurements
    - Brief justification explaining functionality, practicality, and responsiveness under the adaptation scenario

**III. CONTENT Lesson 3.6. Solving Real-World Problems Involving Currency, Time, and Temperature Conversions**

At the end of the lesson, learners should be able to:

1. identify the information and conversion factors needed for currency, time, and temperature conversions;

**IV.**

2. interpret exchange rates, time zones, and temperature scales in given situations;

##### OBJECTIVES

3. perform currency, time, and temperature conversions using appropriate mathematical procedures;
4. solve problems involving currency, time, and temperature conversions in real-world contexts; and
5. apply currency, time, and temperature conversions to authentic situations such as travel planning, business transactions, project management, recipe adjustments, weather reports, scientific experiments, storage conditions, and energy efficiency.

Appreciating Lesson Relevance: Planning Your Dream International Trip This activity establishes the relevance and purpose of the lesson by helping learners recognize the practical applications of mathematics in authentic travel situations. Through the exploration of famous tourist destinations, their corresponding countries, currencies, and temperatures, learners connect prior knowledge with real-world contexts that require mathematical thinking and informed decision- making. At this stage, the activity focuses on helping learners appreciate how mathematical concepts support travel planning, budgeting, scheduling, and preparation rather than requiring extensive computations. Guide learners in recognizing the importance of information such as exchange rates, time differences, and weather conditions in making practical travel decisions. To enhance engagement and contextual understanding, teachers may use printed images, presentation slides, interactive multimedia, or digital learning platforms. Educational technology tools such as online maps, currency converters, world clock applications, and weather forecasting platforms may also be integrated to provide authentic learning experiences. During the processing stage, encourage learners to explain how mathematics helps travelers manage expenses, organize schedules, prepare for varying weather conditions, and make informed decisions. This activity serves as a meaningful bridge between learners' prior knowledge and the lesson's intended learning outcomes by highlighting the relevance of mathematics in everyday life. Processing Questions: 1. Which country would you like to visit, and why? 2. What important information should a traveler gather before visiting another country? 3. Why is it important for travelers to understand currency values and time differences before traveling internationally? 4. In what ways can mathematics help travelers make informed decisions about budgeting, scheduling, and international travel planning? 5. Why is understanding temperature conversion important when traveling or preparing food in another country? |

##### B.1. Presenting Examples

Activity B.1

**Exploring Key Concepts: Travel, Time, and Temperature**

Mathematics is often used in practical situations that involve travel, budgeting, and scheduling. Whether planning a school activity, preparing for international travel, or coordinating schedules across locations, mathematical reasoning helps in making informed decisions. In this activity, you will examine practical situations where map scales, currency conversion, and time zone conversion are applied. *Instructions:*

1. Form groups of 3-4 learners.

##### B. Instituting

2. Read and analyze the assigned scenario. **New Knowledge** 3. Discuss the questions provided with your group.
4. Identify the mathematical concept involved and explain how it applies to the situation.
5. Use digital tools, such as online maps, currency converters, or world clock applications, if needed.
6. Prepare a short presentation, written summary, or visual organizer showing the mathematical concept involved, the information needed, and its practical application.
7. Answer the processing questions after the activity. Scenario 1. Budgeting for International Travel A family is preparing for an international trip to Japan and needs to exchange Philippine pesos for Japanese yen to manage travel expenses. The family plans to exchange ₱25,000 for travel expenses. Suppose the exchange rate is:

1 Philippine Peso (PHP) = 2.60 Japanese Yen (JPY)

The family needs to estimate the equivalent amount in Japanese yen before the trip. Answer the following questions:

1. What information is needed for the conversion?
2. What mathematical operation should be used?
6. If you had a limited travel budget, how would mathematical information such as exchange rates, time differences, and temperature conditions influence your travel decisions?

##### After facilitating the activity, establish a clear connection to the lesson by presenting the content and performance

***standards, learning competencies, specific objectives, and the unit performance task.***

Activity B.1

**Exploring Key Concepts: Travel, Time, and Temperature**

This activity introduces the key concepts of currency conversion, time conversion, and temperature conversion through practical situations that learners may encounter in travel, budgeting, communication, food safety, and daily decision-making. By examining authentic scenarios, learners recognize the value of mathematics in solving realworld problems and making informed choices. To promote collaboration and active participation, the teacher may organize learners into small groups and assign a different scenario for analysis. Learners are encouraged to identify the mathematical concept involved, interpret the given information, discuss possible solutions, and explain how mathematics applies to the situation. The activity provides opportunities for learners to connect prior knowledge with new concepts and to develop critical thinking, problem-solving, communication, and collaborative skills. Through situations involving international travel, virtual meetings, and food storage, learners appreciate the practical applications of mathematics in personal, academic, and future professional contexts. To enrich the learning experience, the teacher may integrate educational technology (EdTech) tools such as online currency converters, world clock applications, temperature conversion calculators, spreadsheet applications, and learning management systems. These tools support computation, visualization, collaboration, and authentic application of mathematical concepts. Throughout the discussion, the teacher should emphasize the importance of accuracy when converting currencies, determining time differences, and converting temperatures, since errors may lead to poor financial decisions, missed schedules, unsafe food storage, and unnecessary energy consumption. Learners should also be encouraged to justify their answers and relate the concepts to their own experiences.

Answer the following questions: 1. What is the time difference between the two locations? 2. How can the corresponding time be determined? 3. Why is accurate time conversion important in virtual meetings? 4. What could happen if the time difference is misunderstood? Scenario 3. Food Storage and Energy Efficiency A school canteen stores perishable goods at 4°C, but a newly installed refrigerator displays temperature in Fahrenheit. Deepening Conceptual Understanding Mathematical concepts such as map scales, currency conversion, and time zone conversion are essential in making informed decisions in everyday situations. These concepts are commonly applied in travel planning, budgeting, online transactions, remittances, and international communication. In this activity, learners will deepen their understanding of these concepts by examining definitions, analyzing examples, and applying mathematical reasoning in practical contexts. Instructions: 1. Examine how each mathematical concept is applied in the given real-life situations. | Activity B.2. Deepening Conceptual Understanding This activity deepens learners' understanding of currency conversion, time zone conversion, and temperature conversion through guided discussion, worked examples, and structured practice. Using authentic situations such as international travel, virtual meetings, remittances, food preparation, and food storage, learners recognize the practical applications of mathematics in everyday life. ##### A. Currency Conversion

Different countries use different currencies for financial transactions. When engaging in international travel, online purchases, educational payments, or remittance transactions, money often needs to be converted from one currency to another through currency

**conversion. Currency conversion is the process of changing the**

value of one currency into its equivalent value in another currency. This process uses an exchange rate, which is the value of one currency in relation to another currency. Exchange rates indicate how much of one currency can be exchanged for a specific amount of another currency. Understanding currency conversion and exchange rates is important for budgeting, making financial decisions, and accurately calculating costs in international transactions.

**Important Note: Exchange rates may change due to economic and**

global factors. For this activity, fixed exchange rates are provided to help you focus on the conversion process.

Example: A learner is preparing to participate in an international youth leadership program in Singapore and needs to exchange PHP15,000 into Singapore dollars (SGD) to cover meals, local transportation, entrance fees, and other travel-related expenses. Suppose the exchange rate is:

1 Singapore Dollar ( Determine the amount of Singapore dollars the learner will receive. Solution: To convert Philippine pesos (PHP) to Singapore dollars (SGD), divide the amount in pesos by the exchange rate. =

15,000

=

41

= 365.85 Answer: The learner will receive approximately and temperature conversion promotes food safety and energy efficiency.

Provide structured note sheets, guided computation templates, conversion charts, calculator support, visual organizers, and pair-assisted tasks to ensure inclusive participation. This activity strengthens conceptual understanding and prepares learners for more independent problem-solving tasks in the succeeding activity.

##### B. Time Zone Conversion

Countries around the world observe different local times based on their geographical locations. A time zone is a region that follows the same standard time. Time differences between locations are determined using Coordinated Universal Time (UTC), a global time standard used as the reference point for world timekeeping. A UTC

**offset indicates the number of hours a location is ahead of or behind**

UTC. Time conversion is the process of determining the equivalent local time in another location by comparing their respective UTC offsets.

Examples of UTC offsets include:

1. Philippines - UTC+8
2. Japan - UTC+9
3. Australia (Sydney) - UTC+10
4. United Kingdom - UTC+0
5. Germany - UTC+1
6. United States (New York) - UTC-5 (standard time)
7. United Arab Emirates - UTC+4

Understanding time zones, UTC offsets, and time conversion is important for coordinating international travel, academic programs, virtual meetings, and communication with individuals in different parts of the world.

Example: A learner from the Philippines will attend an online scholarship interview with an institution in New York City. The interview is scheduled for 10:00 AM New York City time (Eastern Time, UTC-4). Since the learner will attend the interview from the Philippines, which

-----

follows Philippine Time (UTC+8), determine the equivalent local time in the Philippines to ensure timely attendance.

Solution: To determine the equivalent local time in the Philippines, compare the time zones of New York City and the Philippines. Given:

1. New York City: UTC-4
2. Philippines: UTC+8
3. Interview Schedule: 10:00 AM (New York City Time) Step 1: Determine the time difference. Compute the difference between the two time zones: UTC+8 - UTC-4 = 12 hours This means that the Philippines is 12 hours ahead of New York City. Step 2: Adjust the scheduled interview time. Since the Philippines is ahead, add 12 hours to the interview time in New York City:

10:00 AM + 12 hours = 10:00 PM

Answer: The equivalent local time in the Philippines is 10:00 PM Philippine Time. Therefore, the learner should attend the online scholarship interview at 10:00 PM.

*Guided Practice*

1. A learner from the Philippines will attend an online university orientation in Tokyo (UTC+9) scheduled at 3:00 PM. Since the participant will attend from the Philippines, which follows Philippine Time (UTC+8), determine the corresponding local time in the Philippines.
2. A Senior High School student from the Philippines is preparing to submit a research paper to an international student research conference hosted by an institution in California, United States (UTC-7). The submission deadline is 11:59 PM California Time. Since the student will submit the research paper from the Philippines, which follows Philippine Time (UTC+8), determine the corresponding submission deadline in the Philippines.

##### C. Temperature Conversion

Temperature is a measure of the degree of hotness or coldness of an object or environment. It is commonly used in weather forecasting, food preparation, food storage, scientific investigations, and energy management. Different countries use different temperature scales; therefore, understanding temperature conversion is important for interpreting and comparing temperature measurements. The two most used temperature scales are Celsius (°C) and Fahrenheit (°F). The Celsius scale is widely used in the Philippines and many other countries, while the Fahrenheit scale is commonly used in the United States. The reference points of the two temperature scales are shown below:

| Temperature | Celsius | Fahrenheit |
|---|---|---|
| Freezing point of water | 0°C | 32°F |
| Boiling point of water | 100°C | 212°F |

Temperature conversion is the process of determining the equivalent value of a temperature from one scale to another. The following formulas are commonly used: To convert Celsius to Fahrenheit

To convert Fahrenheit to Celsius

When converting temperatures, it is important to:

1. Identify the given temperature scale.
2. Select the appropriate conversion formula.
3. Substitute the given value correctly.
4. Perform the mathematical operations carefully.
5. Express the final answer using the correct unit (°C or °F).

9 = + 32 5

Understanding temperature conversion is important when preparing food using international recipes, interpreting weather reports, maintaining proper food storage conditions, conducting scientific experiments, and managing energy-efficient appliances.

The teacher should encourage learners to present complete and organized solutions, including formulas, computations, and clearly stated final answers using the appropriate units or currency symbols. These technology-enhanced strategies help learners connect mathematical concepts with practical digital tools used in everyday life. This activity also serves as a formative assessment that enables the teacher to monitor learners' mastery of the concepts and identify areas requiring further reinforcement before progressing to more complex application or performance-based tasks.

##### B.1. Presenting Examples

Activity B.1.

**Exploring Key Concepts: Classifying the Functions as Linear or B. Instituting Quadratic Through Graphs New Knowledge**

*Instructions:*

1. Classify the Function as Linear, Quadratic, or Neither
2. The class will be divided into groups.
3. Graphs will be shown one at a time on the board.
4. You have 20 seconds to analyze each graph and decide whether it represents a linear, quadratic, or neither function.
5. After 20 seconds, each group must raise their answer cards. The group with the correct answer gets one point.
6. The group with the highest total score at the end of the game wins!

Activity B.1.

**Exploring Key Concepts: Classifying the Functions as Linear or Quadratic Through Graphs Materials Needed**

- Graph cards or slides (Graphs is provided in the Teacher's Notes or you can provide your own.)
- Mini whiteboards or answer cards .

#### Facilitation Guide:

1. Divide the class into groups and explain the mechanics of the game.
2. Show one graph at a time and give groups 20 seconds to analyze it.
3. Ask groups to raise their answer cards simultaneously.
4. Award one point for each correct answer.
5. Ask learners to briefly explain their answers before revealing the correct classification. *Processing Questions:*
1. What characteristics helped you identify a linear function?
2. What characteristics helped you identify a quadratic function?
3. How do the graphs of linear and quadratic functions differ? Transition to the Lesson

Part II. Properties and Graphs of Quadratic Functions

1. The general form of a quadratic function is where: (a) , (b) , and (c) are constants and a is not equal to zero. (a) determines the direction and width of the parabola.
2. The graph of a quadratic function is a parabola.
3. The vertex is the highest or lowest point on the graph, depending on the direction of the parabola.
        - If (a > 0), the parabola opens upwards, and the vertex is the minimum point.
        - If (a < 0), the parabola opens downwards, and the vertex is the maximum point.
4. The axis of symmetry is a vertical line that passes through the vertex, given by

##### Vertex Form of a Quadratic Function:

Where:

- ((h, k)) is the vertex
- (a) determines the direction and width of the parabola

##### Shortcut Steps to Graph

Step 1. Identify the Vertex

- From the equation, extract ( h ) and ( k )
- Example: (y = (x - 3)^2 + 2) → Vertex is ((3, 2)) Step 2. Plot the Vertex
- This is the turning point of the parabola.

Step 3. Determine the Direction

- From the vertex, move:

| o | 1 unit left/right → 1 unit up/down |
|---|---|
| o | 2 units left/right → 4 units up/down |
| o | 3 units left/right → 9 units up/down |

- Multiply these values by (|a|) if (a ≠ 1). Step 5. Draw the Axis of Symmetry
- A vertical line through ( = ℎ ) Step 6. Sketch the Parabola
- Plot symmetric points on both sides of the vertex
- Connect the points with a smooth curve

##### Vertex:

- Both functions have the same vertex

x - coordinate, which is x = 2.

Activity C.2. Wrapping Up the Lesson: Compare and Conclude *Facilitation Guide:*

Begin by explaining: "Today, you will compare and summarize what you have learned about linear and quadratic functions. You will identify their similarities, differences, and real-life uses to deepen your understanding of mathematical models."

Quadratic Function a. Provide learners with the comparison table or display it on

2. Equation Structure
    - Compare the equations of linear and quadratic functions. What do you notice about the highest power of ?

3. Key Features
    - What are the important features of a linear graph? Of a quadratic graph?
    - Why is the slope important in a linear function?
    - Why is the vertex important in a quadratic function?
4. Rate of Change
    - Describe the rate of change in a linear function.
    - How is the rate of change different in a quadratic function?
5. Real-Life Applications

##### Reflection

*Think about the lessons and activities you completed.*

- *Which type of function was easier for you to understand?* *Why?*
- How do graphs help you understand relationships between *variables?*
- How can linear and quadratic functions help people solve real*life problems?*

##### 4. Highlight Key Ideas

Emphasize the following:

a. Linear functions form straight lines and have constant rate of change.
b. Quadratic functions form parabolas and have changing rates of change.
c. The highest exponent determines the type of function.
d. The slope is important in linear functions.
e. The vertex is important in quadratic functions, especially in optimization problems.

##### 5. Encourage Real-Life Connections

a. electricity bills
b. fundraising profit
c. projectile motion
d. pricing strategies
e. area optimization

##### 6. Conduct Reflection and Synthesis

Allow learners to reflect on:

a. similarities and differences between functions
b. challenges encountered during the lesson
c. practical uses of functions in everyday life

Deepening Conceptual Understanding This activity is designed to develop learners' deep conceptual understanding of cubic, square root, and cube root functions through guided study of definitions, properties, transformations, and graph behavior. It promotes meaningful learning by allowing students to analyze and interpret mathematical representations, including algebraic expressions, tables of values, and graphical sketches. The lesson emphasizes the relationships among domain, range, symmetry, intercepts, and end behavior of functions, helping learners understand how these properties define the shape and behavior of graphs. The activity also supports conceptual mastery of function transformations, including shifts, reflections, and stretches/compressions, enabling learners to connect algebraic |

2. Function: = √ Definition: A square root function is a function that contains a square root with the independent variable in the radicand. The parent function for the family of square root functions is () = √. The domain of is ≥ 0, and the range of is ≥ 0.

##### Key Properties

Domain:

- The domain is ≥ 0.
- Since you cannot take the square root of a negative in the real number system, then the function is defined for non-negative real numbers. Range:

| ● | The range is | ≥ 0. |
|---|---|---|
| ● | The output is | always non-negative real numbers. |

Intercepts:

- x-intercept: (0, 0)
- y-intercept: (0, 0)
- The function passes through the origin. End Behavior:

| ● | As | → ∞,ℎ | → ∞ |
|---|---|---|---|
| ● As | → | 0+,ℎ | → 0 |

-----

- The function is increasing on its entire domain [0, ∞). However, it increases slowly - the rate of increase decreases as x increases.

Symmetry

- The function is neither even nor odd.
- It is not symmetric about the y-axis or origin.

Graph Shape:

- Starts at the origin and curves upward.

Transformation General Form of Transformation:

= √( -ℎ)+ Each parameter transforms the graph in a specific way:

- : Vertical stretch/compression and reflection over the x-axis
- : Horizontal stretch/compression and reflection over the yaxis
- ℎ: Horizontal shift (left/right)
- : Vertical shift (up/down)

![](img_p210_1.png)

Examples of Transformations:

1. Vertical Shift = √ + 3 - Moves the graph up to 3 units.

![](img_p210_2.png)

2. Horizontal Shift = √ - 2 - Moves the graph to the right 2 units.

To facilitate this discussion, the teacher may apply one of the following strategies. Strategy 1. Collaborative Output-Based Learning Strategy *Instructions:*

1. Divide the class into 3 groups and assign each group a function:
      - Group 1: = 3 + 1
      - Group 2: = √
      - Group 3: = √ 3
2. Instruct each group to collaboratively create a function property diagram showing key features such as domain, range, intercepts, symmetry, and graph shape.
3. Let learners use Desmos or GeoGebra to graph their assigned function.
4. Guide learners in analyzing their graph and writing a generalization box containing at least three observations or patterns.
5. Allow each group to present their diagram and explain their findings to the class.
6. Facilitate synthesis and highlight similarities and differences among the functions.

Strategy 2. Technology-Enhanced Presentation Strategy *Instructions:*

1. Assign each group a function and ensure access to digital graphing tools (e.g., Desmos or GeoGebra).
2. Instruct learners to graph their assigned function and explore its properties using the tool.
3. Have each group prepare a short digital presentation (or poster output) showing:
      - Graph of the function
      - Key properties (domain, range, intercepts, shape)
      - Generalization box with at least three observations
4. Let groups present their outputs in class.
5. Encourage peer questioning and clarification during presentations.
6. The teacher summarizes the key learning insights and connects all function behaviors.
